When the lower endpoints grow geometrically backwards through the coordinates, multiplying <a href="#eq:mertens-box" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mertens-box">[eq:mertens-box]</a> costs only a bounded factor; if their sum of errors is small, it is the relative product error.

<a id="stmt-lem-boxes"></a>
<div id="lem:boxes" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:projected-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:box-enlargement"></a>
**Lemma 8**. *Let $0\le j\le L$. Sum over primes $p_1,\ldots,p_j$ whose coordinates satisfy $$0.9\widetilde b_i\le u_i\le1.1\widetilde b_i,\qquad
 (u_1,\ldots,u_j)\in S_j(\boldsymbol\xi).$$ Then $$\label{eq:projected-mass}
 \sum_{p_1,\ldots,p_j}\prod_{i=1}^j\frac1{p_i-1}\ll G_m.$$ Every unit box meeting this region lies in $S_j(\boldsymbol\beta)$, where, with a sufficiently large absolute constant, $$\label{eq:box-enlargement}
 \beta_t=1+C\bigl(\mathrm e^{-h_t/40}+h_t^2\rho^{h_t}\bigr)
 \quad(0\le t<j),\qquad \beta_j=1.$$ This enlargement has bounded volume cost. Its coordinate scaling factors through $R$ differ from $1$ by $O(\mathrm e^{-H/40}+H^2\rho^H)$.*

*There is also the following shell estimate in $L$ coordinates. Fix $A>0$. Among unit boxes meeting the displayed region, retain those with a point satisfying, for some $i\le R$, at least one of these conditions:*

1.  *$i\ge1$ and $u_i$ is within $A$ of a coarse band endpoint;*

2.  *the absolute value of $\xi_i u_i-\sum_{r=i+1}^L a_{r-i}u_r$ is at most $Ah_i^2$;*

3.  *$\sum_{r=i+1}^L a_{r-i}u_r\ge(1-h_i^{-4})u_i$.*

*The total volume of these boxes, and their total reciprocal-prime weight, is <a id="eq:boundary-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:boundary-mass}
 O_A(H^{-2}G_m).$$ The same estimate holds when any subset of coordinates uses reciprocal-prime measure and the others use Lebesgue measure. For conditions (i)–(ii) alone, or for the region lost by replacing the prefix coefficients $\xi_i$, $0\le i\le R$, with $1$, the stronger bound is $$O_A\left(G_m\sum_{h\ge H}
       (h\mathrm e^{-h/40}+h^3\rho^h)\right).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-boxes"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-boxes</summary>

*Proof.* The band lower bounds are $u_t\gg h_t\rho^{-h_t}$. Changing each coordinate by at most $1$ changes inequality $t$ by $O(h_t^2)$. These errors are absorbed by <a href="#eq:box-enlargement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:box-enlargement">[eq:box-enlargement]</a>, including at $t=0$, where $u_0=B$ is fixed. The logarithm of its Jacobian upper bound is at most $$C\sum_{h\ge P}h\bigl(\mathrm e^{-h/40}+h^2\rho^h\bigr)\ll1.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:simplex">6</a>, <a href="#eq:G-uniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:G-uniform">[eq:G-uniform]</a>, and <a href="#eq:mertens-box" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mertens-box">[eq:mertens-box]</a> prove <a href="#eq:projected-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-mass">[eq:projected-mass]</a>. The coordinate scaling factor is $\prod_{t<i}\beta_t$, without the Jacobian exponents; summing for $h_t>h_i\ge H$ proves its stated bound.

For the shells put $h=h_i$. Throughout a box meeting the bands, $u_i\ll b_i$. Conditions (ii) and (iii), together with the unit perturbations, put the enlarged slack in an interval starting at zero of length at most $$C_A\{b_i(h^{-4}+\mathrm e^{-h/40})+h^3\}.$$ Here the $h^3$ term includes $h^2\rho^hu_i=O(h^3)$. Since $Lg_i/B\ll\rho^h$, Lemma <a href="#lem:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:simplex">6</a> bounds the relative volume by $$C_A(h^{-3}+h\mathrm e^{-h/40}+h^3\rho^h).$$ Condition (i), after expanding its interval by $2$, costs only $O_A(\rho^h)$. Sum these estimates over $h\ge H$, and use the bounded enlargement cost. The prime-weight assertion follows again from <a href="#eq:mertens-box" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mertens-box">[eq:mertens-box]</a>. For the stronger assertion, omit $h^{-4}$ from the slack interval. Changing $\xi_i$ to $1$, for $i\le R$, only removes points whose old slack is between $0$ and $(\xi_i-1)u_i$, which obey the same bound. Finally every full unit box has uniformly bounded mass when an arbitrary subset of its coordinates uses reciprocal-prime measure: multiply the factors in <a href="#eq:mertens-box" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mertens-box">[eq:mertens-box]</a> for precisely that subset. This proves the mixed-measure assertion as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-smooth-tail"></a>
<div id="lem:smooth-tail" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:smooth-cost"></a>
**Lemma 9**. *Uniformly for $Y\ge\exp(\exp2)$ and $Z\ge0$, $$\sum_{P^+(a)\le Y}\frac{a^{1/\log Y}}{\varphi(a)}\ll\log Y,
 \qquad
 \sum_{\substack{P^+(a)\le Y\\\log a>Z}}\frac1{\varphi(a)}
 \ll(\log Y)\exp(-Z/\log Y).$$ In particular, with $Y=\exp\exp(1.1\widetilde b_L)$, $$\label{eq:smooth-cost}
 \sum_{P^+(a)\le Y}\frac1{\varphi(a)}\le K_H,\qquad
 \sum_{\substack{P^+(a)\le Y\\\log a>\exp(2\widetilde b_L)}}
 \frac1{\varphi(a)}
 \le K_H\exp\{-\exp(cP\rho^{-P})\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-smooth-tail"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-smooth-tail</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\sigma=1/\log Y$. The Euler factor is $$1+\frac{p^\sigma}{(p-1)(1-p^{\sigma-1})},$$ whose logarithm is $p^{-1+\sigma}+O(p^{-2+2\sigma})$, uniformly in this range of $\sigma$. Partial summation gives $\sum_{p\le Y}(p^{-1+\sigma}-p^{-1})=O(1)$; hence the product is $O(\log Y)$. Rankin’s inequality gives the tail bound. For the last assertion the exponent is $$1.1\widetilde b_L-\exp(0.9\widetilde b_L),$$ and $\widetilde b_L\asymp P\rho^{-P}$. ◻

</details>

Combining Lemmas <a href="#lem:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxes">8</a> and <a href="#lem:smooth-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smooth-tail">9</a>, the shell estimates remain valid after summing a smooth cofactor with weight $1/\varphi(a)$, at a cost of at most $K_H$. In particular their weighted mass is bounded by <a id="eq:weighted-boundary"></a>$$\label{eq:weighted-boundary}
 C K_HG_m\left\{H^{-2}+
       \sum_{h\ge H}(h\mathrm e^{-h/40}+h^3\rho^h)\right\}.$$ The $H^{-2}$ term is needed only for condition (iii).

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Coverage of the values

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-smooth-tail-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-smooth-tail</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:basic">5</a>.* Take $\eta=0.04$ in <a href="#eq:ford-bands" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-bands">[eq:ford-bands]</a>. By <a href="#eq:alpha-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-comparison">[eq:alpha-comparison]</a>, its complementary bands imply the coarse bands in <a href="#eq:basic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:basic">[eq:basic]</a>. Apply <a href="#eq:ford-simplex" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-simplex">[eq:ford-simplex]</a> as well. Both conclusions hold for all preimages outside the union of their exceptional sets.

We may also remove $v\le x^{0.95}$ and $\Omega(v)>5B$, at a cost $o(V(x))$. For the latter, the elementary reciprocal moment bound with $z=1.9<2$ gives $$\#\{v\le x:\Omega(v)>5B\}
 \le z^{-5B}x\sum_{r\le x}\frac{z^{\Omega(r)}}r
 \ll x\exp((z-5\log z)B)=o(x/\log x).$$ The Euler product bounds the reciprocal sum by $O((\log x)^z)$, and $z-5\log z<-1$. For every preimage, $\Omega(n)\le\Omega(\varphi(n))+1\le6B$. The $p_1$ band gives $\log p_1\le(\log x)^{0.61}$, so $$\log p_0\ge0.95\log x-6B(\log x)^{0.61}>0.9\log x.$$

It remains to impose the size restriction on $a$. For any preimage failing it, fix $p_1,\ldots,p_L,a$. The prime $p_0$ is distinct from the others and from the factors of $a$. Supermultiplicativity of $\varphi$ gives $$\varphi(p_0\cdots p_La)
 \ge(p_0-1)\varphi(a)\prod_{i=1}^L(p_i-1).$$ The prime number theorem upper bound therefore gives at most $$\frac{Cx}{\log x}\,
 \frac1{\varphi(a)\prod_{i=1}^L(p_i-1)}$$ choices of $p_0$; if the interval is nonempty its upper endpoint is at least $x^{0.9}$. Sum the prefix by Lemma <a href="#lem:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxes">8</a>, and the cofactor by Lemma <a href="#lem:smooth-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:smooth-tail">9</a>. This bounds all failing preimages, and therefore all values having at least one such preimage. It proves the exceptional-value assertion.

Finally $b_{i+1}/b_i=(1-1/h_i)\rho$. The bands and <a href="#eq:alpha-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:alpha-comparison">[eq:alpha-comparison]</a> imply that the largest double logarithm after index $R$ is at most $0.62b_R$. There are $H-P\le H$ such primes, proving the second line of <a href="#eq:cofactor-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cofactor-size">[eq:cofactor-size]</a>. Its right-hand side is smaller than $\exp(0.89b_R)<\log p_R$ for large $H$, then large $x$. For the first line use the $p_1$ bound and $L\ll\log B$, while the $a$ bound depends only on $H$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Tuples and their discarded part

For $t=x$ or $t=x/c$, where $c>1$ is fixed, keep all parameters formed from $x$. Let $\mathcal T(t)$ be the set of distinct tuples $$\tau=(p_0,\ldots,p_R,d)$$ admitting at least one basic witness with $$d=\varphi(w),\qquad w=p_{R+1}\cdots p_La,\qquad
 d\prod_{i=0}^R(p_i-1)\le t.$$ Each tuple represents a totient, because $w<p_R$ makes its tail coprime to its prefix. Several witnesses may give the same tuple; $\mathcal T(t)$ counts that tuple once.

<a id="eq:good-conditions"></a>
We next remove tuples for which the comparison argument might fail. For each $0\le i\le R$, put $h=h_i$ and $$J_i=\lceil30\log h\rceil,\qquad
 S_i=\exp\exp(b_i^{1/3}),\qquad
 z_i=\exp\exp(0.7b_{i+J_i}),$$ and, for a basic witness, put $$D_i=\varphi(p_{i+J_i+1}\cdots p_La).$$ For large $H$ these indices exist: $J_i<h_i/2$ and $i+J_i<L$. We call a tuple good if *every* basic witness giving it satisfies, for every $0\le i\le R$, $$\label{eq:good-conditions}
 \begin{gathered}
 \sum_{r=i+1}^L a_{r-i}u_r\le(1-h_i^{-4})u_i,\\
 p_i,\ldots,p_{i+J_i}\text{ are }S_i\text{-normal},\\
 (p_i-1)\cdots(p_{i+J_i}-1)
 \text{ is squarefree on primes exceeding }z_i,\\
 \Omega(D_i)\le b_i/h_i^8 .
 \end{gathered}$$ As before, the first condition uses $u_0=B$. Denote the good tuples by $\mathcal G(t)$, and put $\mathcal D(t)=\mathcal T(t)\setminus\mathcal G(t)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-discard"></a>
<div id="prop:discard" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:tail-cutoffs"></a>
**Proposition 10**. *For $t=x$ and $t=x/c$, with the same $x$-dependent parameters, $$|\mathcal D(t)|
 \le\{\varepsilon_H+o_{x;H}(1)\}\frac{xG_m}{\log x}.$$ One may take $\varepsilon_H\ll K_HH^{-2}$. For all the witnesses in <a href="#eq:good-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-conditions">[eq:good-conditions]</a> we also have $$\label{eq:tail-cutoffs}
 P^+(D_i)<z_i,\qquad S_i<z_i,\qquad
 c h_i^{-20}\le b_{i+J_i}/b_i\le C h_i^{-18}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-discard"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-discard</summary>

*Proof.* The last comparison follows from $$\frac{b_{i+J_i}}{b_i}=(1-J_i/h_i)\rho^{J_i},
 \qquad 18<30\log(1/\rho)<20.$$ It gives $S_i<z_i$. The next prime band has upper endpoint, in double logarithms, less than $0.7b_{i+J_i}$; all remaining primes, including those in $a$, are smaller. Taking totients cannot introduce a prime above this bound. This proves <a href="#eq:tail-cutoffs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-cutoffs">[eq:tail-cutoffs]</a>.

A bad tuple has at least one failing full witness. We bound the number of these witnesses; this is an upper bound for the number of bad tuples, regardless of witness multiplicity. The prime-count upper bound for $p_0$ contributes $Cx/\log x$, with the product of reciprocal factors for all other variables. The smooth $a$-sum contributes at most $K_H$. Failure of the first condition in <a href="#eq:good-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-conditions">[eq:good-conditions]</a> is covered by Lemma <a href="#lem:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxes">8</a>, condition (iii). Its contribution is therefore $O(K_HH^{-2}xG_m/\log x)$.

For the other conditions fix $i$, write $h=h_i$, $b=b_i$, and $J=J_i$. If $i\ge1$, the reciprocal sum for the common initial segment $p_1,\ldots,p_{i-1}$ is $O(G_m)$, by <a href="#eq:projected-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-mass">[eq:projected-mass]</a>. All of $p_i,\ldots,p_L$ are at most $\exp\exp(1.2b)$. Their unrestricted reciprocal sums together cost $\exp(Ch^2)$, since there are at most $h$ variables and $\log b=O(h)$. For $i=0$, omit $p_0$ from these reciprocal sums and use the same estimate at $b=B$.

<a id="eq:normal-discard"></a>
Partial summation of <a href="#eq:normal-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normal-primes">[eq:normal-primes]</a> gives $$\sum_{\substack{p\le\exp\exp(1.2b)\\
                   p\ {\rm not}\ S_i\text{-normal}}}\frac1{p-1}
 \ll(1+b)^6\mathrm e^{-b^{1/3}/6}
 \le\mathrm e^{-c b^{1/3}}.$$ For a failure involving $p_0$, apply <a href="#eq:normal-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normal-primes">[eq:normal-primes]</a> directly to its counting interval; its upper endpoint has logarithm comparable with $\log x$. The normality failures thus contribute, relative to $xG_m/\log x$, at most $$\label{eq:normal-discard}
 K_H\sum_{h\ge H}\exp(Ch^2-cb(h)^{1/3}),
 \qquad b(h)=\alpha h\rho^{-h}.$$ Polynomial factors from choosing an index are included in $Ch^2$.

A square of a prime $q>z_i$ in the shifted-prime product either divides one shift twice or divides two different shifts. For $\ell=1,2$ and $Z\ge2$, the elementary estimate $$\sum_{\substack{p\le Z\\q^\ell\mid p-1}}\frac1{p-1}
 \le\frac{1+\log Z}{q^\ell}$$ follows by summing over all positive multiples of $q^\ell$. If the shift is $p_0-1\le T$, its integer count is at most $\lfloor T/q^\ell\rfloor\le T/q^\ell$. Relative to prime counting this loses at most $\log x=\mathrm e^b$ when $i=0$. Both types of square therefore cost $q^{-2}$, up to $\exp(Cb+Ch^2)$. Summing over $q>z_i$, their total relative contribution is at most <a id="eq:square-discard"></a>$$\label{eq:square-discard}
 K_H\sum_{h\ge H}
 \exp\{Ch^2+Cb(h)-\exp(c b(h)h^{-20})\}.$$

<a id="eq:omega-discard"></a>
Finally $\Omega(\varphi(a))\ll\log a\le\mathrm e^{2\widetilde b_L}
=H^{o(1)}$, whereas $b_i/h_i^8$ is exponentially large in $h_i\ge H$. In the identity for $D_i$, overlap between $a$ and the displayed primes can occur only at $p_L$. That overlap changes the sum of factor counts by $1-\Omega(p_L-1)\le0$. Failure of the last condition in <a href="#eq:good-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-conditions">[eq:good-conditions]</a> therefore forces some $i+J<j\le L$ to satisfy $\Omega(p_j-1)>b_i/(2h^9)$. The Euler product gives $$\sum_{n\le Z}\frac{(3/2)^{\Omega(n)}}n\ll(\log Z)^{3/2}.$$ Using the band bound for $p_j$, this exceptional reciprocal sum is at most $$\exp(Cb_{i+J}-cb_i/h^9)\le\exp(-c'b_i/h^9),$$ because $b_{i+J}\ll b_i h^{-18}$. The resulting relative contribution is at most $$\label{eq:omega-discard}
 K_H\sum_{h\ge H}\exp(Ch^2-cb(h)/h^9).$$ Each sum in <a href="#eq:normal-discard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normal-discard">[eq:normal-discard]</a>–<a href="#eq:omega-discard" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:omega-discard">[eq:omega-discard]</a> decreases faster than every inverse power of $H$, uniformly for $\alpha$ in its compact range. Together with the thin-slack bound, this proves the proposition. Replacing the endpoint $x$ by $x/c$ only decreases the witness sets, so the same bound holds with unchanged parameters. ◻

</details>

The two cuts have now served different purposes. The cut at $L$ keeps the smooth-cofactor cost at $K_H=H^{o(1)}$; the earlier cut at $R$ supplies the summable $H^{-2}$ loss and the much stronger savings in the remaining discards. Proposition <a href="#prop:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:basic">5</a> controls exceptional *values*, while Proposition <a href="#prop:discard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:discard">10</a> controls exceptional *tuples*. Neither yet asserts uniqueness of a representation. That is the purpose of the comparison and collision arguments that follow.

<a id="sec:layers"></a>
### Comparing products of shifted primes

We next bound the number of ways that two ordered lists of primes can give the same product of their shifts. The largest prime factors of the shifts lie in separated intervals. We expose the factors one interval at a time, starting with the smallest interval. At each stage only two or three simultaneous prime conditions are needed. This keeps the constants uniform when the number of intervals grows.

The layer decomposition and the comparison of dual factorizations go back to Maier and Pomerance (Maier and Pomerance 1988, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[MaierPomerance1988](#ref-MaierPomerance1988)] and were developed further in Ford’s proof of Lemma 5.1 (Ford 1998) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[FordTotients2013](#ref-FordTotients2013)]. We prove the version needed here, including its dependence on the number of layers. In particular, we assume squarefreeness of the whole product above the last cutoff; squarefreeness of each individual shift would not suffice for our factor-allocation estimate.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### A uniform sieve for two or three forms

<a id="stmt-lem-sieve"></a>
<div id="lem:sieve" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sieve-count"></a>
<a id="eq:sieve-reciprocal"></a>
**Lemma 11**. *There are absolute constants $C$ and $y_0$ with the following property. Let $y\ge y_0$, put $B_y=\log_2 y$, and let $1\le A,A'\le y$ be integers. Consider either the two forms $$n,\quad An+1,$$ or the three forms $$n,\quad An+1,\quad A'n+1,\qquad A\ne A'.$$ Write $k=2$ or $3$, respectively, and let $\mathcal P$ be the set of positive integers at which all the indicated forms are prime. Then, uniformly for $X\ge3$, $$\label{eq:sieve-count}
 \#(\mathcal P\cap[1,X])
 \le C\frac{X B_y^k}{(\log X)^k}.$$ Moreover, uniformly for $3\le U\le Y$, $$\label{eq:sieve-reciprocal}
 \sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal P\\U\le n\le Y}}\frac1n
 \le C\frac{B_y^k}{(\log U)^{k-1}}.$$ The coefficients need not be bounded in terms of $X$ or $U$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sieve"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sieve</summary>

<a id="eq:sieve-determinant"></a>
*Proof.* For primitive, distinct, admissible integral linear forms $a_jn+b_j$ with positive leading coefficients and nonzero determinant $\Delta$ as defined below, Ford’s uniform upper sieve (Ford 2023, Theorem 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[FordSieve2023](#ref-FordSieve2023)] gives $$\label{eq:sieve-determinant}
 \begin{gathered}
 \#\{1\le n\le X:a_jn+b_j\text{ prime for every }j\}
 \ll_k\frac{X}{(\log X)^k}
 \left(\frac{\Delta}{\varphi(\Delta)}\right)^k,\\
 \Delta=\left|\prod_j a_j
       \prod_{j<l}(a_jb_l-a_lb_j)\right|.
 \end{gathered}$$ Here admissibility means that no prime divides the product of the forms for every integer input; the implied constant depends only on $k$, including when the coefficients vary. For our forms, primitivity is automatic, and the determinants are $A$ and $AA'(A-A')$, up to sign. They are nonzero and have absolute value at most $y^3$.

If our forms are inadmissible, an offending prime is at most $k$, since each primitive linear form has at most one root modulo that prime. At every simultaneous-prime input one of the forms must equal this prime. Each such equality has at most one solution, so there are at most $k$ inputs. Their contribution is absorbed in <a href="#eq:sieve-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sieve-count">[eq:sieve-count]</a>.

For admissible forms, the elementary estimate $$\frac{d}{\varphi(d)}\ll\log_2(d+10)\qquad(d\ge1)$$ and <a href="#eq:sieve-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sieve-determinant">[eq:sieve-determinant]</a> prove <a href="#eq:sieve-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sieve-count">[eq:sieve-count]</a>. To recall its uniformity, split the product over prime divisors of $d$ at $\log(d+10)$. Mertens’ product estimate bounds the first part by $O(\log_2(d+10))$. For the remaining part, use $\sum_{p\mid d}\log p\le\log d$ to bound the sum of reciprocal prime divisors, giving a bounded factor. Since $k$ takes only the values $2$ and $3$, all constants are absolute.

Finally, partial summation of <a href="#eq:sieve-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sieve-count">[eq:sieve-count]</a>, with the coefficients fixed, gives $$\sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal P\\U\le n\le Y}}\frac1n
 \ll B_y^k\left(\frac1{(\log Y)^k}
       +\int_U^Y\frac{\,\mathrm dt}{t(\log t)^k}\right)
 \ll\frac{B_y^k}{(\log U)^{k-1}}.$$ This also includes a possible prime at the lower endpoint. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### The comparison estimate

For a positive integer $n$ and a real number $Z$, its part supported above $Z$ means $\prod_{p^a\parallel n,\ p>Z}p^a$. We use $S$-normality as defined in Definition <a href="#def:normal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:normal">3</a>.

The key term in the bound below is the exponent of $\log y$. In its application, a strict simplex inequality makes the weighted cutoff sum plus its error smaller than $1$ by a controlled amount. The exponent is consequently smaller than $-1$; that extra saving absorbs the other factors, even after recovering the tuple data hidden in the smooth remainder. We need an estimate for ordered pairs with multiplicity: a bound only on the number of values admitting a collision would not control how many tuples lie over those values.

<a id="stmt-prop-comparison"></a>
<div id="prop:comparison" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12**. *There are absolute constants $C$ and $y_0$ such that the following holds. Let $y\ge y_0$, put $B_y=\log_2 y$, and let $b$ be an integer with $1\le b\le B_y$. Suppose that real cutoffs satisfy $$Y_0=y,\qquad \mathrm e^\mathrm e\le S\le Y_b,
 \qquad Y_{j+1}<U_j<Y_j\quad(0\le j<b).$$ Put $$\nu_j=\frac{\log_2 Y_j}{B_y}\quad(0\le j\le b),
 \qquad
 \mu_j=\frac{\log_2 U_j}{B_y}\quad(0\le j<b),
 \qquad
 \delta=\sqrt{\frac{\log_2 S}{B_y}}.$$ Fix a positive integer $D$ with $P^+(D)\le Y_b$ and a real $r\ge1$. Let $N$ count the ordered tuples $$(p_0,\ldots,p_{b-1},q_0,\ldots,q_{b-1},D')$$ in which $D'$ is a positive integer and the following conditions hold:*

1.  *The $p_j,q_j$ are $S$-normal primes, $p_j\ne q_j$, and $$U_j\le P^+(p_j-1),P^+(q_j-1)\le Y_j
      \qquad(0\le j<b).$$*

2.  *$P^+(D')\le Y_b$ and $$D\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}(p_j-1)
      =D'\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}(q_j-1)\le\frac yr.$$*

3.  *The common product in <span class="upright">(ii)</span> is squarefree on primes greater than $Y_b$.*

4.  *The part of $p_0-1$ supported above $Y_1$ exceeds $\sqrt y$.*

<a id="eq:comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:comparison-error"></a>
*Then $$\label{eq:comparison}
 \begin{split}
 N\le{}&\frac{y}{rD}(CB_y^6)^b(b+1)^{\Omega(D)}
       (\log Y_b)^{1+3b\log(b(b+1))}\\
 &\quad\times
 (\log y)^{-2+\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}a_j\nu_j+E_b},
 \end{split}$$ where $$\label{eq:comparison-error}
 E_b=2\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}(\nu_j-\mu_j)
      +\delta\sum_{i=2}^b(i\log i+i).$$ Here $a_j$ is as defined in <a href="#eq:aj" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:aj">[eq:aj]</a>. Empty sums are zero; in particular, when $b=1$ the cutoff in <span class="upright">(iv)</span> is $Y_1=Y_b$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* We first describe the layers and the weighted summation that combines them. We then count the top layer, estimate a general lower layer, and finally sum over the small prime factors.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### The layer configurations.

<a id="eq:layer-two-allocations"></a>
For $0\le i\le b$ and $0\le j<b$, let $$s_{i,j}=\prod_{\substack{p^a\parallel p_j-1\\p\le Y_i}}p^a,
 \qquad
 s'_{i,j}=\prod_{\substack{p^a\parallel q_j-1\\p\le Y_i}}p^a.$$ Restricting the equality in condition <span class="upright">(ii)</span> to primes at most $Y_i$ gives the common product $$s_i^\#=D\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}s_{i,j}
        =D'\prod_{j=0}^{b-1}s'_{i,j}.$$ For $1\le i\le b$, put $$t_{i,j}=\frac{s_{i-1,j}}{s_{i,j}},\qquad
 t'_{i,j}=\frac{s'_{i-1,j}}{s'_{i,j}},\qquad
 t_i^\#=\frac{s_{i-1}^\#}{s_i^\#}.$$ Each $t_i^\#$ is squarefree and supported on $(Y_i,Y_{i-1}]$. For $j\ge i$ the factors $t_{i,j}$ and $t'_{i,j}$ equal $1$, so $$\label{eq:layer-two-allocations}
 t_i^\#=\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}t_{i,j}
        =\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}t'_{i,j}.$$ Thus a layer consists of two allocations of the same squarefree integer among $i$ labeled slots.

Let $\mathcal C_i$ be the set of configurations $$\sigma_i=(D',s_{i,0},\ldots,s_{i,b-1},
                   s'_{i,0},\ldots,s'_{i,b-1})$$ arising from tuples counted by $N$. Restriction of prime factors maps $\mathcal C_{i-1}$ into $\mathcal C_i$; the two allocations in <a href="#eq:layer-two-allocations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:layer-two-allocations">[eq:layer-two-allocations]</a> determine each extension uniquely. Reconstructing a tuple from its small prime factors runs from $\mathcal C_b$ back to $\mathcal C_0$; the weighted count below sums in the opposite direction, starting with the top layer. The original tuples are in bijection with $\mathcal C_0$, since $p_j=s_{0,j}+1$ and $q_j=s'_{0,j}+1$. For $i\ge2$ we will bound, uniformly in $\sigma_i$, the reciprocal extension sum $$\sum_{\substack{\sigma_{i-1}\in\mathcal C_{i-1}\\
                  \sigma_{i-1}\text{ restricts to }\sigma_i}}
       \frac1{t_i^\#}.$$ This is the appropriate weight because $1/s_{i-1}^\#=1/(s_i^\#t_i^\#)$.

Every configuration considered here extends to a genuine solution. In particular, its partial common product is at most $y/r$, each shift is at most $y$, and every fixed or conditional coefficient used below is at most $y$. When enlarging a sum for an upper bound, we retain any necessary restriction on a conditional coefficient until the corresponding sieve estimate has been applied.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### The top layer.

<a id="eq:top-layer"></a>
Only the zeroth shift can have a prime factor greater than $Y_1$. Consequently $t_1^\#$ is the same part of $p_0-1$ and $q_0-1$, and $t_1^\#>\sqrt y$. Normality, applied with endpoint $p_0-1$, gives $$\Omega(p_0-1)
 \le B_y+\log_2 S+\sqrt{B_y\log_2 S}\le3B_y.$$ Write $t_1^\#=t'Q$, with $Q=P^+(t_1^\#)$. The weaker bound $\Omega(t_1^\#)\le4B_y$ already gives $$Q\ge y^{1/(8B_y)}.$$ After $\sigma_1$ and $t'$ are fixed, the completed primes have the forms $AQ+1,A'Q+1$. Their inequality implies $A\ne A'$, which is exactly the nonzero determinant condition for the three-form sieve. With $$X=\frac{y}{r s_1^\#t'},$$ a nonempty extension has $X\ge Q\ge y^{1/(8B_y)}$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sieve">11</a> therefore bounds the number of $Q$ by $$\ll\frac{X B_y^3}{(\log X)^3}
 \ll\frac{y}{r s_1^\#t'}\frac{B_y^6}{(\log y)^3}.$$ The integer $t'$ is squarefree and supported on $(Y_1,y]$. Mertens’ estimate gives $$\sum_{t'}\frac1{t'}
 \le\prod_{Y_1<p\le y}\left(1+\frac1p\right)
 \ll\frac{\log y}{\log Y_1}.$$ It follows that, for each $\sigma_1$, $$\label{eq:top-layer}
 \#\{\sigma_0\text{ restricting to }\sigma_1\}
 \le CB_y^6\frac{y}{r s_1^\#}
       (\log y)^{-2-\nu_1}.$$

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### The prime conditions in an intermediate layer.

Fix $2\le i\le b$ and $\sigma_i$. The last slot, of index $i-1$, is complete after this layer. Let $Q_1,Q_2$ be its largest prime factors on the two sides; both lie in $[U_{i-1},Y_{i-1}]$.

<a id="eq:shared-layer-prime"></a>
Suppose first that $Q_1=Q_2=Q$. Remove the unique occurrence of $Q$ from $t_i^\#$, and call the result $t$. For fixed allocations of $t$, the completed primes are $AQ+1,A'Q+1$ with $A\ne A'$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sieve" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sieve">11</a> gives $$\label{eq:shared-layer-prime}
 \sum_Q\frac1Q\ll\frac{B_y^3}{(\log U_{i-1})^2}.$$

<a id="eq:distinct-layer-primes"></a>
Now suppose $Q_1\ne Q_2$ and remove both, obtaining $t$. In addition to its two allocations, fix the position of $Q_1$ on the second side and of $Q_2$ on the first side. There are at most $i^2$ choices. If neither prime lies in the opposite last slot, the two completed prime conditions are $AQ_1+1$ and $A'Q_2+1$; two applications of <a href="#eq:sieve-reciprocal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sieve-reciprocal">[eq:sieve-reciprocal]</a> with $k=2$ give $$\label{eq:distinct-layer-primes}
 \sum_{Q_1,Q_2}\frac1{Q_1Q_2}
 \ll\frac{B_y^4}{(\log U_{i-1})^2}.$$ If $Q_2$ lies in the last slot on the first side, then $Q_2<Q_1$. The prime $Q_1$ cannot lie in the last slot on the second side, whose largest prime is $Q_2$. The completed prime conditions are now $AQ_2Q_1+1$ and $A'Q_2+1$. First sum over $Q_1$ conditional on $Q_2$, using the forms $Q_1,AQ_2Q_1+1$. Feasibility ensures $AQ_2\le y$, so the inner reciprocal sum is uniformly $O(B_y^2/\log U_{i-1})$. Only after this estimate do we enlarge the range of $Q_2$ and apply the two-form bound to $Q_2,A'Q_2+1$. This proves <a href="#eq:distinct-layer-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distinct-layer-primes">[eq:distinct-layer-primes]</a> again. The reversed incidence is treated by reversing the order of summation. Both incidences cannot occur simultaneously, as they would imply both $Q_1<Q_2$ and $Q_2<Q_1$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Counting the allocations.

<a id="eq:allocation-entropy"></a>
Put $\Delta_i=\nu_{i-1}-\nu_i$. Normality bounds the number of prime factors of an individual shift in $(Y_i,Y_{i-1}]$ by $(\Delta_i+\delta)B_y$. Indeed, if the shift ends before $Y_{i-1}$, apply normality only up to its endpoint; if it ends before $Y_i$, the count is zero. In either case the error is at most $\sqrt{\log_2 S\,B_y}=\delta B_y$. Only $i$ shifts contribute to this layer, and removing the distinguished primes decreases the count. Thus $$\Omega(t)\le I_i:=i(\Delta_i+\delta)B_y.$$ Since $t$ is squarefree, each of its prime factors chooses one of $i$ slots on each side, giving at most $i^{2\Omega(t)}$ dual allocations. Write $$M_i=\sum_{Y_i<p\le Y_{i-1}}\frac1p.$$ Mertens’ estimate gives $M_i\le\Delta_i B_y+O(1)$, uniformly in the cutoffs. Since $\delta B_y\ge\sqrt{B_y}$, increasing the absolute threshold $y_0$ ensures $M_i\le(\Delta_i+\delta)B_y$. Hence $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_t\frac{i^{2\Omega(t)}}t
 &\le\sum_{n\le I_i}\frac{(i^2M_i)^n}{n!}
 \le i^{I_i}\exp(iM_i)\notag\\
 &\le\exp\bigl((i\log i+i)(\Delta_i+\delta)B_y\bigr).
 \label{eq:allocation-entropy}
\end{aligned}$$ The middle inequality follows by writing $i^{2n}=i^ni^n$, using $i^n\le i^{I_i}$, and extending the remaining exponential series. It is valid without any lower bound on $\Delta_i/\delta$.

<a id="eq:intermediate-layer"></a>
Combining <a href="#eq:shared-layer-prime" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shared-layer-prime">[eq:shared-layer-prime]</a>, <a href="#eq:distinct-layer-primes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distinct-layer-primes">[eq:distinct-layer-primes]</a>, and <a href="#eq:allocation-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:allocation-entropy">[eq:allocation-entropy]</a>, and using $i\le b\le B_y$ to absorb the $i^2$ position choices, gives $$\label{eq:intermediate-layer}
 \sum_{\substack{\sigma_{i-1}\text{ restricting}\\
                  \text{to }\sigma_i}}\frac1{t_i^\#}
 \le CB_y^6
 (\log y)^{-2\mu_{i-1}+(i\log i+i)(\nu_{i-1}-\nu_i+\delta)}.$$ The squarefree hypothesis was used both to remove a single occurrence of each distinguished prime and to obtain the factorial denominator in <a href="#eq:allocation-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:allocation-entropy">[eq:allocation-entropy]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
###### Multiplying the layer estimates.

Starting from <a href="#eq:top-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:top-layer">[eq:top-layer]</a>, sum over $\mathcal C_1$. For each subsequent layer use the exact weighted identity $$\sum_{\sigma_{i-1}\in\mathcal C_{i-1}}\frac1{s_{i-1}^\#}
 =\sum_{\sigma_i\in\mathcal C_i}\frac1{s_i^\#}
   \sum_{\sigma_{i-1}\text{ restricting to }\sigma_i}
          \frac1{t_i^\#}.$$ All bounds in <a href="#eq:intermediate-layer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intermediate-layer">[eq:intermediate-layer]</a> are uniform in the lower configuration. Their constants therefore multiply to at most $C^bB_y^{6b}$. If $c_i=i\log i+i$, so that $c_1=1$, the power of $\log y$ accumulated in this procedure is $$\begin{aligned}
 &-2-\nu_1+
   \sum_{i=2}^b\{-2\mu_{i-1}+c_i(\nu_{i-1}-\nu_i+\delta)\}\\
 &\qquad=-2+\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}a_j\nu_j
     +2\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}(\nu_j-\mu_j)
     +\delta\sum_{i=2}^b c_i-c_b\nu_b.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $c_{j+1}-c_j-2=a_j$, including $j=1$. The identity also holds for $b=1$. Discard the nonpositive final term $-c_b\nu_b$. It remains to bound $\sum_{\sigma_b\in\mathcal C_b}1/s_b^\#$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### The smooth parts and repeated factors.

Write $s=s_b^\#/D$ and $L_b=\log_2Y_b$. For a fixed $s$, its allocation among the $b$ left slots has at most $b^{\Omega(s)}$ possibilities. The allocation of $sD$ among the $b$ right slots and the additional slot $D'$ has at most $(b+1)^{\Omega(sD)}$ possibilities. These bounds allow prime powers: assigning labeled copies of a repeated prime to slots overcounts every possible distribution of its exponent.

Each left shift has largest prime factor above $Y_b$. Its number of prime factors at most $Y_b$ is bounded by normality as follows: $$\Omega(p_j-1,1,Y_b)
 \le 2\log_2 S+(L_b-\log_2 S)
       +\sqrt{L_b\log_2 S}\le3L_b.$$ If $S=Y_b$, the first normality inequality alone gives the same bound. Thus $\Omega(s)\le3bL_b$. The ordinary smooth-number Euler product, including all powers, now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\sigma_b\in\mathcal C_b}\frac1{s_b^\#}
 &\le\frac{(b+1)^{\Omega(D)}}D
   [b(b+1)]^{3bL_b}
   \sum_{P^+(s)\le Y_b}\frac1s\\
 &=\frac{(b+1)^{\Omega(D)}}D
   [b(b+1)]^{3bL_b}
   \prod_{p\le Y_b}\left(1-\frac1p\right)^{-1}\\
 &\ll\frac{(b+1)^{\Omega(D)}}D
   (\log Y_b)^{1+3b\log(b(b+1))}.
\end{aligned}$$ No squarefreeness is assumed below $Y_b$. Combining this estimate with the layer bounds proves <a href="#eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison">[eq:comparison]</a>, after increasing the absolute constant $C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-comparison-uniformity"></a>
<div id="rem:comparison-uniformity" class="remark">

**Remark 13**. The explicit error in <a href="#eq:comparison-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-error">[eq:comparison-error]</a> satisfies $$E_b=2\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}(\nu_j-\mu_j)
       +O\bigl(\delta b^2\log(2b)\bigr),$$ and the smooth-factor exponent is $O(b\log(2b))$. Thus all dependence on a growing $b$ is displayed in <a href="#eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison">[eq:comparison]</a>. No restriction such as $r\le y^{1/10}$ or $D\le y^{1/100}$ is needed: the large top part supplies the lower bound for the actual sieve endpoint.

</div>

<a id="sec:collisions"></a>
### Uniqueness of the continuous prefix

We now pass from representations to distinct totient values. We retain the tuple sets $\mathcal T(t)$, $\mathcal G(t)$ and $\mathcal D(t)$ of Section <a href="#sec:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:extraction">3</a>: the first consists of the distinct tuples $(p_0,\ldots,p_R,d)$ admitting a basic witness, the second consists of those for which every basic witness satisfies <a href="#eq:good-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-conditions">[eq:good-conditions]</a>, and $\mathcal D(t)=\mathcal T(t)\setminus\mathcal G(t)$. Their value map is $$F(p_0,\ldots,p_R,d)=d\prod_{j=0}^R(p_j-1).$$ The tail preimages of $d$ are not part of a tuple. This distinction is essential: we shall prove that almost every value has one such tuple, while allowing several tail preimages.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Counting pairs with the same value

The alignment of largest factors and cancellation follow the argument around (5.34)–(5.37) in Ford’s Section 5 (Ford 1998) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[FordTotients2013](#ref-FordTotients2013)]. Here we count all ordered pairs of distinct tuples, including the data inside both residual factors. We first cancel the common prefix and align the large factors in the remaining shifts. After applying the comparison estimate, we recover the tuple coordinates inside the residual factors and then restore the common prefix.

<a id="stmt-prop-collisions"></a>
<div id="prop:collisions" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14** (Prefix collisions). *Fix $c>1$, and form all parameters from $x$, with $H$ fixed before $x$ tends to infinity. For $t=x$ or $t=x/c$, let $$K(t)=\#\{(\tau,\sigma)\in\mathcal G(t)^2:
             \tau\ne\sigma,\ F(\tau)=F(\sigma)\}.$$ There is a quantity $\epsilon_H\to0$, independent of the phase, such that $$K(t)\le\bigl(\epsilon_H+o_{x;H}(1)\bigr)
                   \frac{xG_m}{\log x}.$$ The pairs counted here are ordered pairs of distinct tuples.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-collisions"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-collisions</summary>

<a id="eq:suffix-identity"></a>
*Proof.* Choose one basic witness for each tuple under consideration. All choices satisfy <a href="#eq:good-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-conditions">[eq:good-conditions]</a>, by the definition of $\mathcal G(t)$. If two distinct tuples have the same value, some displayed prime differs: equality of all the primes $p_0,\ldots,p_R$ would also force equality of $d$. Let $i$ be the first unequal prime index, and write $q_j$ for the primes in the other witness. Put $$h=m-i,\qquad J=\lceil30\log h\rceil,\qquad
 z=z_i=\exp\exp(0.7b_{i+J}),\qquad S=S_i=\exp\exp(b_i^{1/3}).$$ Thus $h\ge H$. The residual integers in the good conditions are $$D_i=\varphi(p_{i+J+1}\cdots p_La),\qquad
 D_i'=\varphi(q_{i+J+1}\cdots q_La').$$ Canceling the common prefix leaves the identity $$\label{eq:suffix-identity}
 v^{(i)}=D_i\prod_{j=0}^{J}(p_{i+j}-1)
        =D_i'\prod_{j=0}^{J}(q_{i+j}-1).$$ We will apply Proposition <a href="#prop:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparison">12</a> to this identity. First we verify its size and interval hypotheses; then we count all ways in which its solutions can give tuple pairs.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Sizes of the suffix and its layers.

<a id="eq:collision-scales"></a>
For sufficiently large $H$, uniformly for $h\ge H$, $$\label{eq:collision-scales}
 b_i\asymp h\rho^{-h},\qquad
 \log b_i=O(h),\qquad
 c_1h^{-20}\le\frac{b_{i+J}}{b_i}
       =(1-J/h)\rho^J\le C_1h^{-18}.$$ Indeed $30\lambda$ lies strictly between $18$ and $20$; the ceiling in $J$ changes only constant factors. In particular $J<h/2$ and $i+J<L$. The constants in this proof are independent of $H,x,i$ and the phase, once $H$ and then $x$ are sufficiently large.

<a id="eq:suffix-sizes"></a>
For $i=0$ set $y=x$. For $i\ge1$ place $v^{(i)}$ in a dyadic interval $(y/2,y]$. The coarse bands in <a href="#eq:basic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:basic">[eq:basic]</a> imply that the logarithm of the preimage suffix after $p_i$ is at most $$h\exp(0.62b_i)+\exp(2\widetilde b_L).$$ To see the first term, use $1.1\widetilde b_{i+1}<0.62b_i$ for large $x$, and bound the number of displayed factors by $h$. The bound for $a$ gives the second term. These quantities are negligible compared with $\log p_i\ge\exp(0.88b_i)$. Consequently, writing $B_y=\log_2y$, $$\label{eq:suffix-sizes}
 p_i,q_i\ge y^{0.9},\qquad
 0.88b_i\le B_y\le1.12b_i,\qquad u_i\le B_y+O(1).$$ For $i=0$ the first inequality is part of the basic witness conditions, and $B_y=b_0=u_0=B$ by convention.

Our target is a bound of the form $$\frac{y}{\log y}\exp(-\kappa B_y/h^4)$$ for the suffix pairs, with an absolute $\kappa>0$. The strict simplex slack will supply the exponential saving. Since $B_y\asymp b_i$, this saving can absorb the $\exp(O(b_i\log h/h^8))$ cost of recovering tuple coordinates inside $D_i$ and $D_i'$.

<a id="eq:largest-factor-band"></a>
Let $$\delta=\sqrt{\log_2S/B_y}\asymp b_i^{-1/3}.$$ It follows from <a href="#eq:collision-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collision-scales">[eq:collision-scales]</a> that $S<z$ and $J\delta=o(h^{-20})$. Normality gives $\Omega(p_{i+j}-1),\Omega(q_{i+j}-1)\le4B_y$ for $0\le j\le J$. For example, split the prime factors at $S$, apply the two normality conditions, and use $p_{i+j}-1\le v^{(i)}\le y$. If $$A_j=P^+(p_{i+j}-1),\qquad A_j'=P^+(q_{i+j}-1),$$ then $$\label{eq:largest-factor-band}
 \log_2A_j=u_{i+j}+O(\log b_i),\qquad
 \log_2A_j'=\log_2q_{i+j}+O(\log b_i).$$ This follows from $(p_{i+j}-1)\le A_j^{\Omega(p_{i+j}-1)}$ and the reverse bound $A_j\le p_{i+j}-1$. When $i=j=0$, the convention $u_0=B$ causes only an $O(1)$ difference from $\log_2p_0$, since $x^{0.9}\le p_0\le x+1$.

The bands in <a href="#eq:largest-factor-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:largest-factor-band">[eq:largest-factor-band]</a> are disjoint and decrease with $j$. Their smallest separation is a constant multiple of $b_i h^{-20}$, which dominates the error $O(\log b_i)$. All the $A_j,A_j'$ exceed $z$, whereas $$P^+(D_i),P^+(D_i')<z.$$ For the latter assertion, every prime in the residual preimage is at most $p_{i+J+1}$ or $q_{i+J+1}$, and $1.1\widetilde b_{i+J+1}<0.7b_{i+J}$; taking a totient cannot increase the largest prime factor. Repetitions of the last prime in $a$ or $a'$ do not affect this conclusion.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Aligning the largest factors on the two sides.

<a id="eq:factor-alignment"></a>
We claim that $$\label{eq:factor-alignment}
 |\log_2 A_j-\log_2 A_j'|\le(2j+1)\delta B_y
                 \qquad(0\le j\le J).$$ Suppose, for example, that $A_j>A_j'$, and put $e=(\log_2 A_j-\log_2 A_j')/B_y$. On the first side of <a href="#eq:suffix-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:suffix-identity">[eq:suffix-identity]</a>, each of its first $j+1$ shifts contributes at least $(e-\delta)B_y$ prime factors, with multiplicity, to $(A_j',A_j]$. On the other side only its first $j$ shifts can contribute, each at most $(e+\delta)B_y$. The later shifts have smaller largest factors, and both residual integers are $z$-smooth. The lower endpoint exceeds $S$, so normality applies. Equality of the two sides therefore gives $$(j+1)(e-\delta)\le j(e+\delta),$$ which proves <a href="#eq:factor-alignment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:factor-alignment">[eq:factor-alignment]</a>. Interchanging the sides handles the other order.

Cancel any equal primes among the two lists in <a href="#eq:suffix-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:suffix-identity">[eq:suffix-identity]</a>, and let $r$ be the product of their shifts. The disjoint prime bands show that equality is possible only at the same original index. The index $i$ survives, since $p_i\ne q_i$. Let $b\le J+1\le B_y$ be the length of the remaining lists. Their corresponding primes are unequal, as required by Proposition <a href="#prop:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparison">12</a>.

Set $Y_b=z$, $Y_0=y$ and $\log_2U_0=0.8B_y$. At each remaining positive index, choose an interval on a $\delta$-grid in normalized double logarithms which contains the corresponding pair from <a href="#eq:factor-alignment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:factor-alignment">[eq:factor-alignment]</a>. Its width is $O(J\delta)$. The intervals remain strictly separated, because $J\delta=o(h^{-20})$. Each lies below height $0.8$, by <a href="#eq:suffix-sizes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:suffix-sizes">[eq:suffix-sizes]</a> and the upper bound $u_{i+1}\le0.62b_i$. At index zero, <a href="#eq:suffix-sizes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:suffix-sizes">[eq:suffix-sizes]</a> and <a href="#eq:largest-factor-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:largest-factor-band">[eq:largest-factor-band]</a> give largest factors above height $0.9$. Thus all the ordered-cutoff hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparison">12</a> hold. If $b=1$, there are no positive indices to grid and $Y_1=z$.

The part of $p_i-1$ supported on primes at most $Y_1$ has logarithm at most $4B_y\log Y_1\le4B_y\exp(0.8B_y)$. Since $p_i\ge y^{0.9}$, the complementary part exceeds $\sqrt y$ for large $H$. The whole common product is squarefree above $Y_b$, by the good conditions; canceling the equal shifts preserves this property. This verifies the last two hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#prop:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:comparison">12</a>.

Apply that proposition to the remaining lists with $D=D_i$, $D'=D_i'$ and the factor $r$ just canceled; their common product is at most $y/r$. Write $\nu_k=\log_2Y_k/B_y$ and $\mu_k=\log_2U_k/B_y$ for the normalized interval endpoints.

<a id="eq:collision-slack"></a>
Write $j_1<\cdots<j_{b-1}$ for the surviving positive original indices. Since $j_k\ge k$ and $a_k\le a_{j_k}$, the grid intervals and the strict slack condition in <a href="#eq:good-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:good-conditions">[eq:good-conditions]</a> give $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{k=1}^{b-1}a_k\nu_k
 &\le \frac{1}{B_y}\sum_{j=1}^{J}a_j u_{i+j}
                          +O(J^2\log(2J)\delta)\notag\\
 &\le1-h^{-4}+O(B_y^{-1}+J^2\log(2J)\delta).
 \label{eq:collision-slack}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $a_j$ is increasing and $\sum_{j\le J}a_j=O(J\log(2J))$. The grid widths and the explicit $\delta$ term in <a href="#eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison">[eq:comparison]</a> contribute another $O(J^2\log(2J)\delta)=o(h^{-4})$ to its exponent of $\log y$. Thus, for some absolute $\kappa>0$, its logarithmic factor is at most $$(\log y)^{-1-\kappa h^{-4}}
 =\frac1{\log y}\exp(-\kappa B_y/h^4).$$ It remains to show that the other factors and the recovery of tuple coordinates use only a smaller part of this saving.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Recovering tuples and summing the remaining choices.

<a id="eq:residual-factorization"></a>
<a id="eq:tuple-recovery-cost"></a>
The comparison estimate counts its displayed primes and its residual integer $D_i'$. We must also account for the tuple data forgotten inside $D_i,D_i'$. If $R\le i+J$, all displayed tuple primes are already determined. Their tail totient is $$d=D_i\prod_{j=R+1}^{i+J}(p_j-1),$$ and similarly on the other side, with an empty product when $R=i+J$. If $R>i+J$, the missing tuple data give an ordered factorization $$\label{eq:residual-factorization}
 D_i=d\prod_{j=i+J+1}^{R}(p_j-1).$$ There are at most $(h+1)^{\Omega(D_i)}$ such factorizations. Indeed, for any integer $n$, assigning its $\Omega(n)$ prime occurrences to $k$ labeled slots bounds the number of ordered multiplicative factorizations into $k$ factors by $k^{\Omega(n)}$. Once a factor $p_j-1$ is fixed, $p_j$ is fixed as well. This argument also covers repeated prime factors. Consequently the two missing tails cost at most $$\label{eq:tuple-recovery-cost}
 (h+1)^{\Omega(D_i)+\Omega(D_i')}
       \le\exp\{2b_i\log(h+1)/h^8\}.$$ The factorizations in <a href="#eq:residual-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-factorization">[eq:residual-factorization]</a> are exact: the primes up to $R$ are distinct and the remaining preimage tail is smaller than $p_R$, by <a href="#eq:cofactor-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cofactor-size">[eq:cofactor-size]</a>. All repetitions inside that tail are kept in its totient $d$.

We now sum the comparison bound over the canceled primes, the grids, and $D_i$. The canceled index sets contribute at most $2^{J+1}$. Each canceled prime has reciprocal shift sum $O(b_i)$ by Mertens’ theorem. The number of grid choices is at most $(C/\delta)^{2(J+1)}$. Thus their total cost, including the factors $(CB_y^6)^b$ in <a href="#eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison">[eq:comparison]</a>, is $\exp(O(h^2))$. For the residual integer, the finite Euler product gives $$\sum_{P^+(D)\le z}\frac1D\ll\log z.$$ Before enlarging this sum we use the good-condition bounds $\Omega(D_i),\Omega(D_i')\le b_i/h^8$ in both <a href="#eq:tuple-recovery-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tuple-recovery-cost">[eq:tuple-recovery-cost]</a> and the factor $(b+1)^{\Omega(D_i)}$ of the comparison estimate.

<a id="eq:suffix-pair-count"></a>
The logarithm of all the remaining costs, apart from the factor $y/(\log y)$ and the saving from <a href="#eq:collision-slack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collision-slack">[eq:collision-slack]</a>, is at most <a id="eq:collision-costs"></a>$$\label{eq:collision-costs}
 O(h^2)+O\left(\frac{b_i\log h}{h^8}\right)
       +O\left(\frac{b_i\log h\log\log(3h)}{h^{18}}\right)
       =o\left(\frac{b_i}{h^4}\right).$$ The last term uses $\log_2z\ll b_i h^{-18}$ to bound the factor $(\log Y_b)^{1+3b\log(b(b+1))}$ in <a href="#eq:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison">[eq:comparison]</a>, as well as the sum over $D_i$. All little-oh estimates here are uniform for $h\ge H$ as $H\to\infty$, because $b_i\asymp h\rho^{-h}$. We have therefore obtained, for some absolute $c_2>0$, $$\label{eq:suffix-pair-count}
 \#\{\text{ordered suffix tuple pairs with }y/2<v^{(i)}\le y\}
 \ll\frac{y}{\log y}\exp\{-c_2B_y/h^4\}.$$ For $i=0$ the same expression bounds all the pairs with first unequal index zero, using $y=x$. Choosing one witness for each tuple has not introduced multiplicity into the desired count: comparison data and the factorizations just counted determine each ordered pair of tuples.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Summing suffixes and restoring the common prefix.

<a id="eq:dyadic-suffix-mass"></a>
For $i\ge1$ the required sum over suffix pairs is weighted by $1/v^{(i)}$. A dyadic interval in <a href="#eq:suffix-pair-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:suffix-pair-count">[eq:suffix-pair-count]</a> contributes at most $$\frac{C}{\log y}\exp\{-c_2\log_2y/h^4\}.$$ Writing the dyadic endpoints as $y=2^n$, and comparing the sum with an integral, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\text{suffix pairs}}\frac1{v^{(i)}}
 &\ll\sum_{\log(n\log2)\ge0.88b_i-O(1)}
       \frac{\exp\{-c_2\log(n\log2)/h^4\}}{n\log2}\notag\\
 &\ll\int_{0.88b_i-O(1)}^\infty\exp(-c_2u/h^4)\,\,\mathrm du
  \ll h^4\exp(-c_3b_i/h^4).
 \label{eq:dyadic-suffix-mass}
\end{aligned}$$ In this change of variable, $u=\log(n\log2)$, the factor $1/\log y$ exactly compensates for the density of dyadic endpoints. In particular, no factor of order $\exp(b_i)$ is lost.

For fixed suffix data and common primes $p_1,\ldots,p_{i-1}$, the number of possible $p_0$ is at most $$\frac{Cx}{\log x}\,
 \frac1{v^{(i)}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}(p_j-1)}.$$ Indeed the upper endpoint for $p_0$ is $1+x/(v^{(i)}\prod_{j=1}^{i-1}(p_j-1))$; if the range is nonempty, its restriction $p_0\ge x^{0.9}$ makes its logarithm comparable to $\log x$, so the prime number theorem gives this upper bound. The projected-prefix estimate <a href="#eq:projected-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-mass">[eq:projected-mass]</a> and <a href="#eq:dyadic-suffix-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dyadic-suffix-mass">[eq:dyadic-suffix-mass]</a> now show that the contribution for this $i$ is at most $$\frac{CxG_m}{\log x}\,h^4\exp(-c_3b_i/h^4).$$ This also applies to the empty projected prefix when $i=1$. Summing over $h\ge H$ yields the convergent tail $$C\sum_{h\ge H}h^4\exp\{-c_4\rho^{-h}/h^3\}\longrightarrow0.$$ For $i=0$, <a href="#eq:suffix-pair-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:suffix-pair-count">[eq:suffix-pair-count]</a> gives $O((x/\log x)\exp\{-c_2B/m^4\})$, which is $o(xG_m/\log x)$. This proves the proposition, uniformly for both endpoints $t$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### From tuples to values and least preimages

The following elementary lemma keeps track of exceptional tuples as well as exceptional values. This is needed because a small set of values could otherwise carry many representations.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-map"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-map" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (Finite-map counting). *Let $T$ and $W$ be finite sets, let $F:T\to W$, and write $T=G\sqcup D$. Suppose that every element of $W\setminus E$ is in $F(T)$, where $E\subset W$. For $w\in W$ put $r_w=|G\cap F^{-1}(w)|$, and let $$K=\sum_{w\in W}r_w(r_w-1).$$ Then $$-|E|\le |T|-|W|\le |D|+K/2.$$ Moreover, with $E^*=E\cup F(D)\cup\{w:r_w\ge2\}$, one has $$|E^*|\le|E|+|D|+K/2,\qquad
 |F^{-1}(E^*)|\le|E|+2|D|+K.$$ The restriction of $F$ to $T\setminus F^{-1}(E^*)$ is a bijection onto $W\setminus E^*$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-map"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-map</summary>

*Proof.* Coverage gives the lower bound. For every positive integer $r$, $r-1\le r(r-1)/2$, and hence $$|G|-|F(G)|\le K/2.$$ Adding the at most $|D|$ remaining elements proves the upper bound. There are at most $K/2$ fibers with $r_w\ge2$, which proves the bound for $|E^*|$. Also $$\sum_{w\in E^*}r_w\le|E^*|+K/2,$$ by $r\le1+r(r-1)/2$ for integers $r\ge0$. Adding the elements of $D$ proves the second bound. Outside $E^*$ coverage, absence of discarded elements, and $r_w\le1$ give exactly one preimage under $F$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-unique-prefix"></a>
<div id="prop:unique-prefix" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:least-factorization"></a>
**Proposition 16** (A common prefix for all preimages). *For $t=x$ or $t=x/c$, with the same parameters formed from $x$, <a id="eq:tuple-cardinality"></a>$$\label{eq:tuple-cardinality}
 \bigl|\,|\mathcal T(t)|-V(t)\,\bigr|
       \le\bigl(\epsilon_H+o_{x;H}(1)\bigr)\frac{xG_m}{\log x},
 \qquad\epsilon_H\longrightarrow0.$$ There is a set $E^*(t)\subset\mathcal V\cap[1,t]$ for which both $|E^*(t)|$ and the number of tuples in $\mathcal T(t)$ with values in $E^*(t)$ are at most $(\epsilon_H+o_{x;H}(1))xG_m/\log x$, after enlarging $\epsilon_H$. For every $v\in(\mathcal V\cap[1,t])\setminus E^*(t)$ there is a unique tuple $(p_0,\ldots,p_R,d)\in\mathcal T(t)$ of value $v$, and every preimage of $v$ has this prefix and a remaining factor of totient $d$. In particular, $$\label{eq:least-factorization}
 \ell(v)=p_0\cdots p_R\ell(d).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-unique-prefix"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-unique-prefix</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-map" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-map">15</a> with $T=\mathcal T(t)$, $G=\mathcal G(t)$, $D=\mathcal D(t)$ and $W=\mathcal V\cap[1,t]$. Let $E$ be the exceptional values in Proposition <a href="#prop:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:basic">5</a>, restricted to $[1,t]$. Every preimage of each $v\notin E$ is basic, so it induces an element of $T$. The size of $E$ is negligible on the scale $xG_m/\log x$, by <a href="#eq:ford-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-scale">[eq:ford-scale]</a>; Proposition <a href="#prop:discard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:discard">10</a> gives the same bound for $|D|$, and Proposition <a href="#prop:collisions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:collisions">14</a> gives it for $K$. The counting conclusions therefore follow from the lemma.

Fix $v\notin E^*(t)$. Every preimage is basic and so induces a tuple in $T$. It cannot induce a tuple in $D$, since $v\notin F(D)$. The remaining tuple is unique. Thus, writing $Q=p_0\cdots p_R$, every preimage of $v$ has the form $Qz$, where $\varphi(z)=d$. It follows that every such preimage is at least $Q\ell(d)$.

For the reverse inequality choose one basic witness with tail $w$. The deterministic size bound <a href="#eq:cofactor-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cofactor-size">[eq:cofactor-size]</a> gives $\ell(d)\le w<p_R$. The prefix primes are distinct, so $\gcd(Q,\ell(d))=1$ and $$\varphi(Q\ell(d))=\varphi(Q)\varphi(\ell(d))
                 =d\prod_{j=0}^R(p_j-1)=v.$$ Hence $\ell(v)\le Q\ell(d)$, proving <a href="#eq:least-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:least-factorization">[eq:least-factorization]</a>. ◻

</details>

The universal quantifier on preimages in Proposition <a href="#prop:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:basic">5</a> is used only after the counting argument, to obtain the common-prefix statement and <a href="#eq:least-factorization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:least-factorization">[eq:least-factorization]</a>. A single basic preimage would give the upper bound for $\ell(v)$ but would not give the reverse inequality.

<a id="sec:limits"></a>
### The arithmetic coefficient and the limiting formula

The preceding sections reduce the count of values to distinct tuples with a common long prime prefix. We now sum over the largest prime and replace the remaining prefix by a volume. The shorter tail stays discrete: its different witnesses are combined by a union before taking that volume. Throughout this section, $H$ is fixed first, $x$ tends to infinity next, and $H$ tends to infinity last. In particular, $P=\lfloor\log\log H\rfloor$, $R=m-H$, and $L=m-P$ always refer to the two cuts in <a href="#eq:cuts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cuts">[eq:cuts]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Summing over the largest prime

<a id="eq:mass"></a>
Let $\mathcal D_H(x)$ be the set of distinct data $(d,p_1,\ldots,p_R)$ for which there are primes $p_{R+1},\ldots,p_L$ and a positive integer $a$ satisfying the basic conditions <a href="#eq:basic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:basic">[eq:basic]</a> with $p_0$ omitted, and $$d=\varphi(w),\qquad w=p_{R+1}\cdots p_La.$$ The convention $u_0=B$ is retained in these conditions. Thus the inequality with index zero is a condition on the displayed data and their witness, independent of the eventual choice of $p_0$. For any function $f:[1,\infty)\to[0,1]$, define $$M_f(x;H)=
 \sum_{(d,p_1,\ldots,p_R)\in\mathcal D_H(x)}
 \frac{f(\ell(d)/d)}{d\prod_{i=1}^R(p_i-1)}.
 \label{eq:mass}$$ Each datum is included once, regardless of its number of witnesses. The function $f=1$ makes the least-preimage ratio irrelevant to this definition.

<a id="eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant"></a>
<a id="eq:mass-upper"></a>
We use $K_H=\exp(CP\rho^{-P})=H^{o(1)}$, allowing the absolute constant $C$ to increase in upper bounds. Summing over witnesses, the totient inequality $$\frac{1}{\varphi(p_{R+1}\cdots p_La)}
 \le \frac{1}{\varphi(a)}
       \prod_{i=R+1}^L\frac{1}{p_i-1}
 \label{eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant}$$ and Lemma <a href="#lem:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxes">8</a> give $$0\le M_f(x;H)\le M_1(x;H)\ll K_HG_m.
 \label{eq:mass-upper}$$ Indeed, the reciprocal sum for the full $L$ prime coordinates is $O(G_m)$ by <a href="#eq:projected-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:projected-mass">[eq:projected-mass]</a>, while the unrestricted reciprocal sum for the allowed smooth cofactor is $O(K_H)$ by <a href="#eq:smooth-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:smooth-cost">[eq:smooth-cost]</a>. The inequality in <a href="#eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant">[eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant]</a>, rather than an equality, also covers the possibility that $p_L$ divides $a$.

<a id="stmt-prop-mass"></a>
<div id="prop:mass" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:mass-count"></a>
**Proposition 17** (The unweighted prime sum). *For each fixed $c>1$, both $t=x$ and $t=x/c$ satisfy $$V(t)=\frac{t}{\log x}
 \bigl(M_1(x;H)+E_H(t;x)G_m\bigr),
 \qquad
 \lim_{H\to\infty}\limsup_{x\to\infty}
 \max_{t\in\{x,x/c\}}|E_H(t;x)|=0.
 \label{eq:mass-count}$$ All parameters in the two formulas are formed from the same $x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-mass</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed data in $\mathcal D_H(x)$ put $$D=d\prod_{i=1}^R(p_i-1).$$ Since $d\le w$, the deterministic bound <a href="#eq:cofactor-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cofactor-size">[eq:cofactor-size]</a> implies $$1\le D\le p_1\cdots p_La\le\exp((\log x)^{0.8}).$$ The possible largest primes are precisely $x^{0.9}\le p_0\le 1+t/D$. The remaining prime factors are smaller than $x^{0.9}$ for large $x$, so this choice introduces neither a repeated prefix prime nor another ordering condition. The endpoints $t/D$ lie uniformly in $[x^{1-o(1)}/c,x]$. The prime number theorem, with $\log(t/D)=\log x+o(\log x)$ uniformly, therefore gives $$\#\{p_0:x^{0.9}\le p_0\le1+t/D\}
 =\frac{t}{D\log x}\bigl(1+o_{x\to\infty;H,c}(1)\bigr).$$ The lower cutoff costs a relative $O(x^{-0.1+o(1)})$, uniformly in the data. Summing this formula and using <a href="#eq:mass-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-upper">[eq:mass-upper]</a> counts $\mathcal T(t)$ with main term $tM_1/\log x$ and error $o_{x\to\infty;H,c}(xG_m/\log x)$. Proposition <a href="#prop:unique-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:unique-prefix">16</a> replaces $\#\mathcal T(t)$ by $V(t)$ with an error whose normalized iterated limit is zero. Since $t$ is either $x$ or $x/c$, this proves <a href="#eq:mass-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-count">[eq:mass-count]</a>. ◻

</details>

The common mass in Proposition <a href="#prop:mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mass">17</a> already gives the fixed-scaling conclusion. Its proof does not require identifying that mass with the arithmetic coefficient.

<a id="stmt-cor-fixed-scaling"></a>
<div id="cor:fixed-scaling" class="corollary">

**Corollary 18** (Fixed scaling). *For every fixed real $c>0$, $$\frac{V(cx)}{V(x)}\longrightarrow c.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-fixed-scaling"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-fixed-scaling</summary>

*Proof.* First let $c>1$. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mass">17</a> at $t=x$ and $t=x/c$, using the same $M_1(x;H)$ and $G_m(x)$. Subtracting gives $$\frac{(V(x)-cV(x/c))\log x}{xG_m}
 =E_H(x;x)-E_H(x/c;x).$$ Ford’s estimate <a href="#eq:ford-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-scale">[eq:ford-scale]</a> supplies a positive constant $c_-$ with $V(x)\log x/(xG_m)\ge c_-$ for large $x$. Consequently $$\limsup_{x\to\infty}
 \left|1-\frac{cV(x/c)}{V(x)}\right|
 \le\frac1{c_-}\limsup_{x\to\infty}
       \bigl(|E_H(x;x)|+|E_H(x/c;x)|\bigr).$$ Let $H$ tend to infinity and then replace $x$ by $cx$ to obtain the assertion. This also covers arguments at which $m(x)$ and $m(x/c)$ differ: the latter integer never enters the comparison. Neither continuity of the phase coefficient nor matching values at the ends of the phase interval is required.

The case $c=1$ is immediate. If $0<c<1$, apply the result just proved to the multiplier $1/c>1$ and the argument $y=cx$; taking reciprocals gives the stated limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### A finite tail and a continuous prefix

The phase-dependent witness set in <a href="#eq:AH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:AH">[eq:AH]</a> changes with the phase, but for fixed $H$ all its members belong to one finite set. This observation will permit uniform limits without any continuity assumption.

<a id="stmt-lem-finite-tail-data"></a>
<div id="lem:finite-tail-data" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:tail-D-bound"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Uniformly finite tail data). *For each sufficiently large fixed $H$, the union of all $\mathcal W_{H,s}(d)$, over $0\le s<1$ and all $d$, is finite. All its prime and integer coordinates, and all its totient values, have explicit bounds depending only on $H$. Uniformly over these data, $$0\le D(h,\eta)\ll_H\log(2h)\qquad(h\ge H).
 \label{eq:tail-D-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-finite-tail-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-finite-tail-data</summary>

*Proof.* Write $a_*=\lambda/\rho$, so that $\lambda\le\alpha_s<a_*$. Let $$q_H=\left\lceil\exp\exp(1.1a_*H\rho^{-H})\right\rceil,
 \qquad
 A_H^*=\left\lceil\exp\exp(2a_*P\rho^{-P})\right\rceil.$$ Every witness belongs to the finite set $\{1,\ldots,q_H\}^{H-P}\times\{1,\ldots,A_H^*\}$. Its corresponding integer $w$ and totient $d$ satisfy $d\le w\le A_H^*q_H^{H-P}$. These bounds do not involve $x$ or $s$. Finally, $a_j=\int_j^{j+1}\log t\,\,\mathrm dt\le\log(j+1)$, so the definition <a href="#eq:tail-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-D">[eq:tail-D]</a> and the bounded tail coordinates give <a href="#eq:tail-D-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-D-bound">[eq:tail-D-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

For $s=\theta(x)$ and $\eta\in\mathcal W_{H,s}(d)$, define the region $\mathcal K_\eta\subset\mathbb R^R$ by $u_0=B$ and <a id="eq:prefix-region"></a>$$u_i-\sum_{r=i+1}^Ra_{r-i}u_r\ge D(m-i,\eta)
 \qquad(0\le i\le R).
 \label{eq:prefix-region}$$ These inequalities imply $u_i\ge0$. The coordinates $u_i$ here are real; the coarse prime bands have been removed. We write $\operatorname{Vol}_R$ for $R$-dimensional Lebesgue measure.

<a id="stmt-lem-tail-volume"></a>
<div id="lem:tail-volume" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:mass-volume"></a>
<a id="eq:intersection-volume"></a>
**Lemma 20** (The volume of the witness union). *There are numbers $\delta_H\to0$ such that, uniformly for $f:[1,\infty)\to[0,1]$, $$\limsup_{x\to\infty}
 \left|\frac{M_f(x;H)}{G_m}
 -\frac1{G_m}\sum_{d:\mathcal W_{H,\theta}(d)\ne\varnothing}
 \frac{f(\ell(d)/d)}d
 \operatorname{Vol}_R
 \bigcup_{\eta\in\mathcal W_{H,\theta}(d)}\mathcal K_\eta
 \right|\le\delta_H.
 \label{eq:mass-volume}$$ Moreover, for every nonempty finite $T\subseteq\mathcal W_{H,\theta}(d)$, $$\operatorname{Vol}_R\bigcap_{\eta\in T}\mathcal K_\eta
 =G_R\left(1-\frac1B\sum_{h=H}^m g_{m-h}
       \max_{\eta\in T}D(h,\eta)\right)_+^R.
 \label{eq:intersection-volume}$$ Here $z_+=\max(z,0)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tail-volume"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tail-volume</summary>

*Proof.* An intersection imposes the largest of the witness thresholds at each slack coordinate.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### Intersection volumes.

Set $C_i=\max_{\eta\in T}D(m-i,\eta)$ and introduce the prefix slacks $$t_i=u_i-\sum_{r=i+1}^Ra_{r-i}u_r\qquad(0\le i\le R).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:simplex" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:simplex">6</a> gives $\sum_{i=0}^Rg_it_i=B$, and the substitution from $(u_1,\ldots,u_R)$ to $(t_1,\ldots,t_R)$ has determinant one. The intersection is $t_i\ge C_i$ for every $i$, including $i=0$. Translation by $C_i$ leaves a simplex of available size $B-\sum_i g_iC_i$. Its volume is $$\frac{(B-\sum_{i=0}^Rg_iC_i)_+^R}
 {R!\prod_{i=1}^Rg_i},$$ which proves <a href="#eq:intersection-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intersection-volume">[eq:intersection-volume]</a> after putting $h=m-i$. It remains to compare the volume of the union with the prime mass.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
###### From primes to volume.

<a id="eq:perturbed-prefix-region"></a>
Identify $Q_h=p_{m-h}$ for $P\le h<H$. These are exactly the primes with indices $R+1,\ldots,L$. For fixed tail witness $\eta$, the original prefix conditions are its coarse bands together with $$\xi_i u_i-\sum_{r=i+1}^Ra_{r-i}u_r\ge D(m-i,\eta)
 \qquad(0\le i\le R).
 \label{eq:perturbed-prefix-region}$$ Taking the union over $\eta\in\mathcal W_{H,\theta}(d)$ is essential: a prime prefix with several witnesses contributes just once to <a href="#eq:mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass">[eq:mass]</a>.

First replace the reciprocal prime sum by the volume of this union with its bands and its $\xi_i$. In a unit box wholly contained in the union, the product of the prime reciprocal sums differs from its volume by a relative $O(\sum_{i=1}^R e^{-c b_i})=O(e^{-c' b_R})$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxes">8</a>. The geometric growth of $b_i$ as $i$ decreases makes this estimate independent of $R$. Both the total mass and the total volume before this replacement are $O(K_HG_m)$, by summing over witnesses as in <a href="#eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant">[eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant]</a>.

If a unit box meets the union without being contained in it, choose a witness whose region meets the box. That witness’s region does not contain the box. Consequently the box lies in a shell at a prefix band endpoint, or within $O(h_i^2)$ of equality in one of <a href="#eq:perturbed-prefix-region" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:perturbed-prefix-region">[eq:perturbed-prefix-region]</a>. For an upper bound, sum these shells over witnesses using <a href="#eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant">[eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant]</a>. Thicken the discrete tail coordinates into unit boxes as well and sum $1/\varphi(a)$ at a cost $O(K_H)$. The resulting full $L$-coordinate boxes are covered by the shell bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxes">8</a> and Proposition <a href="#prop:discard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:discard">10</a>. Their contribution is $o_H(1)G_m$. More explicitly, the slice coefficient for an index with $h=m-i$ is $Lg_i/B\ll\rho^h$. Summing the shell bounds therefore costs at most $$O\!\left(K_H\sum_{h\ge H}
       (h e^{-h/40}+h^3\rho^h)\right)G_m.$$ The broader $O(K_HH^{-2})G_m$ shell estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:discard" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:discard">10</a> also suffices.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
###### Removing the slack perturbation.

Replace $\xi_i$ by $1$ for $i\le R$, retaining the bands. The removed region lies in a shell of thickness $O(e^{-h_i/40}b_i)$, with the same box-thickening errors. Its normalized contribution is at most $$O\!\left(K_H\sum_{h\ge H}
       (h e^{-h/40}+h^3\rho^h)\right)=o_H(1).$$ Both shell estimates are valid for the union because upper bounds may sum over witnesses, while $0\le f\le1$.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
###### Removing the prefix bands.

For every point satisfying the now unperturbed prefix inequalities, recurrence iteration gives <a id="eq:unbanded-prefix-lower"></a>$$u_i\ge g_{R+1-i}v_{H-1}\gg H\rho^{-h_i}
 \qquad(0\le i\le R),
 \label{eq:unbanded-prefix-lower}$$ where $v_{H-1}=\log_2Q_{H-1}$. To see this, retain the contribution of the first tail coordinate in each prefix inequality, discard the other nonnegative tail contributions, and apply the recurrence for $g$. The tail band for $v_{H-1}$ and <a href="#eq:g-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:g-asymptotic">[eq:g-asymptotic]</a> give the displayed lower bound. Thickening the $H-P$ tail coordinates changes inequality $i$ by $O(H\log(h_i+1))$. The enlargement term $C h_i^2\rho^{h_i}u_i$ is at least a constant times $CHh_i^2$, so it absorbs this error. Together with the retained tail bands, this places the full region inside the enlarged simplex of Lemma <a href="#lem:boxes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:boxes">8</a>.

To sum these regions without a factor for the number of witnesses, use <a href="#eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant">[eq:tail-reciprocal-majorant]</a> and first fix $a$. Partition the tail coordinates into half-open unit boxes $\mathcal C=\prod_{h=P}^{H-1}\mathcal C_h$. Let $\mathcal U_{\mathcal C}$ be the union of $\mathcal K_\eta$ over all allowed witnesses $\eta=((Q_h),a)$ with $(\log_2Q_h)_h\in\mathcal C$. Let $E$ be the set of prefix points violating at least one removed band. Aggregating the reciprocal weights within a box gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{\substack{\eta=((Q_h),a)\text{ allowed}\\
                   (\log_2Q_h)_h\in\mathcal C}}
 \frac{\operatorname{Vol}_R(\mathcal K_\eta\cap E)}
      {\prod_h(Q_h-1)}\\
 &\quad\le
 \left(\prod_{h=P}^{H-1}
       \sum_{\log_2q\in\mathcal C_h}\frac1{q-1}\right)
       \operatorname{Vol}_R(\mathcal U_{\mathcal C}\cap E)\\
 &\quad\ll\operatorname{Vol}_L
       \bigl((\mathcal U_{\mathcal C}\cap E)\times\mathcal C\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ The sums over $q$ are over primes. The product is $O(1)$ uniformly in the number of tail coordinates, by <a href="#eq:mertens-box" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mertens-box">[eq:mertens-box]</a> and their geometrically growing lower endpoints. Here $R+(H-P)=L$ and $\mathcal C$ has unit volume. By the preceding enlargement argument, every set on the right lies in the same enlarged $L$-simplex. Since the tail boxes are disjoint, summing their volumes counts a subset of that simplex once. Finally, summing $1/\varphi(a)$ over the common smooth envelope costs $O(K_H)$. All these bounds hold at each phase; they do not compare witness sets at nearby phases.

Scale that enlarged simplex into the true $L$-coordinate simplex. Its Jacobian cost is bounded, and its coordinate scaling factors through index $R$ are $$1+O\!\left(\sum_{h\ge H}
       (e^{-h/40}+h^2\rho^h)\right)=1+o_H(1).$$ For large $H$, and then large $x$, a violation of a removed $[0.9\widetilde b_i,1.1\widetilde b_i]$ band therefore becomes a violation of the $[0.95b_i,1.05b_i]$ band in the true simplex. Lemma <a href="#lem:concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:concentration">7</a> applies with dimension $L=m-P$, since $h_i\ge H$ and $P/H\to0$. Summing its bounds gives $O(e^{-cH})$ of the simplex volume. After the smooth-cofactor factor, the additional volume is at most $O(K_He^{-cH})G_m=o_H(1)G_m$. This proves <a href="#eq:mass-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-volume">[eq:mass-volume]</a>. All constants used in these estimates are uniform in $\theta$, since $\lambda\le\alpha_\theta<\lambda/\rho$ and $\alpha/\alpha_\theta=1+o_{x\to\infty;H}(1)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### The arithmetic approximation

<a id="stmt-prop-tail-limit"></a>
<div id="prop:tail-limit" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:uniform-approximation"></a>
**Proposition 21** (Approximation by the finite coefficient). *There are numbers $\varepsilon_H\to0$ such that, for every $f:[1,\infty)\to[0,1]$, $$\limsup_{x\to\infty}
 \left|\frac{M_f(x;H)}{G_m}-A_H(f;\theta(x))\right|
 \le\varepsilon_H.
 \label{eq:uniform-approximation}$$ The bound is independent of $f$ and of phase. Each $A_H(f;s)$ is a finite sum involving values of $f$ at explicitly bounded rational arguments and absolutely convergent series in its exponential weights, and $A_H(f;s)\ge0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-tail-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-tail-limit</summary>

*Proof.* Apply finite inclusion–exclusion to the union in <a href="#eq:mass-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass-volume">[eq:mass-volume]</a>, and then use <a href="#eq:intersection-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intersection-volume">[eq:intersection-volume]</a>. For a nonempty subset $T$ of witnesses, write $$C_T(h)=\max_{\eta\in T}D(h,\eta),\qquad
 S_T=\sum_{h=H}^{\infty}\rho^hC_T(h),\qquad
 q_{m,T}=\frac1B\sum_{h=H}^mg_{m-h}C_T(h).$$ The series for $S_T$ converges absolutely by <a href="#eq:tail-D-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-D-bound">[eq:tail-D-bound]</a>. The finite universe in Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-tail-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-tail-data">19</a> gives a uniform bound on the number of possible witnesses and their subsets for fixed $H$; it also makes all the following estimates uniform in phase, even when membership in a witness set changes.

Using $B=\alpha m\rho^{-m}$ and <a href="#eq:g-asymptotic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:g-asymptotic">[eq:g-asymptotic]</a>, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 q_{m,T}
 &=\frac{\gamma}{\alpha m}
       \sum_{h=H}^m\rho^h C_T(h)
       +O_H\!\left(\frac{m\log(2m)}B\right)\\
 &=\frac{\gamma}{\alpha m}S_T+o_{x\to\infty;H}(m^{-1}).
\end{aligned}$$ The omitted geometric tail is $O_H(\rho^m\log(2m))$. In particular, $q_{m,T}=O_H(1/m)$, and hence $$(1-q_{m,T})_+^{m-H}
 =\exp\!\left(-\frac\gamma{\alpha_\theta}S_T\right)
       +o_{x\to\infty;H}(1).$$ Here we used the uniform scale relation $\alpha/\alpha_\theta=1+O(\log m/m)$ and $(m-H)q_{m,T}^2=O_H(1/m)$. The prefactor satisfies <a id="eq:volume-prefactor-limit"></a>$$\frac{G_{m-H}}{G_m}
 =\prod_{l=0}^{H-1}\frac{(m-l)g_{m-l}}B
 =\left(\frac\gamma{\alpha_\theta}\right)^H
    \rho^{H(H-1)/2}+o_{x\to\infty;H}(1).
 \label{eq:volume-prefactor-limit}$$ There are only boundedly many terms for fixed $H$. Thus the inclusion–exclusion expression differs from $A_H(f;\theta(x))$ in <a href="#eq:AH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:AH">[eq:AH]</a> by $o_{x\to\infty;H}(1)$, uniformly in phase; no witness multiplicity has been introduced. Together with Lemma <a href="#lem:tail-volume" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tail-volume">20</a>, this proves <a href="#eq:uniform-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uniform-approximation">[eq:uniform-approximation]</a>.

Nonnegativity follows from the finite-union probability interpretation in <a href="#eq:tail-events" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tail-events">[eq:tail-events]</a>: both the union probabilities and the outer weights are nonnegative. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Uniform convergence and the asymptotic equivalent

The preceding approximation holds for every bounded nonnegative weight. Convergence as $H$ grows requires a comparison quantity that does not depend on $H$. For $f=1$ this quantity is the normalized count of totients. Section <a href="#sec:companion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:companion">7</a> will supply a different comparison quantity for each $f_k$.

<a id="stmt-lem-coefficient-convergence"></a>
<div id="lem:coefficient-convergence" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:common-comparison"></a>
<a id="eq:comparison-limit"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Convergence from a common comparison). *Fix $f:[1,\infty)\to[0,1]$. Suppose a real function $C_f(x)$, independent of $H$, and numbers $\eta_H\to0$ satisfy $$\limsup_{x\to\infty}
 \left|C_f(x)-\frac{M_f(x;H)}{G_m}\right|\le\eta_H.
 \label{eq:common-comparison}$$ Then $A_H(f;s)$ converges uniformly for $0\le s<1$ to the limit $A(f;s)$ defined in Section <a href="#sec:statements" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:statements">2</a>, and $$C_f(x)=A(f;\theta(x))+o(1).
 \label{eq:comparison-limit}$$ If additionally $c_-\le C_f(x)\le c_+$ for all sufficiently large $x$, then $c_-\le A(f;s)\le c_+$ for every $s\in[0,1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-coefficient-convergence"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coefficient-convergence</summary>

*Proof.* By Proposition <a href="#prop:tail-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:tail-limit">21</a>, $$\limsup_{x\to\infty}
 |C_f(x)-A_H(f;\theta(x))|\le\zeta_H,
 \qquad \zeta_H=\eta_H+\varepsilon_H\longrightarrow0.$$ Every phase occurs at arbitrarily large real arguments. Indeed, $$\psi(B)=\frac{\log B-\log\log B}{\lambda}$$ is continuous and strictly increasing for $B>e$, and tends to infinity. For any $s\in[0,1)$ solve $\psi(B_n)=n+s$ and put $x_n=\exp(\exp B_n)$. Then $m(x_n)=n$ and $\theta(x_n)=s$ exactly.
