Comparing indices $H$ and $K$ along this same sequence gives $$|A_H(f;s)-A_K(f;s)|\le\zeta_H+\zeta_K.$$ This bound is independent of $s$, so the functions form a uniformly Cauchy sequence. In particular, $\sup_s|A_H(f;s)-A(f;s)|\le\zeta_H$. Combining this inequality with the preceding limsup bound and then sending $H$ to infinity proves <a href="#eq:comparison-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-limit">[eq:comparison-limit]</a>. If $C_f$ lies between $c_-$ and $c_+$, apply those bounds on each exact-phase sequence and let $H$ grow to obtain the asserted bounds for $A$. No continuity in the phase was used. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-coefficient-convergence-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coefficient-convergence</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Set $$C_1(x)=\frac{V(x)\log x}{xG_m}.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:mass">17</a> with $t=x$ supplies <a href="#eq:common-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:common-comparison">[eq:common-comparison]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:coefficient-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coefficient-convergence">22</a> therefore proves uniform existence of $A(1;s)$ and $C_1(x)=A(1;\theta(x))+o(1)$. Ford’s two-sided estimate <a href="#eq:ford-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-scale">[eq:ford-scale]</a> supplies positive absolute constants $c_-,c_+$ bounding $C_1$. The same lemma gives $$0<c_-\le A(1;s)\le c_+<\infty\qquad(0\le s<1).$$ The additive approximation is consequently equivalent to $$V(x)\sim\frac{x}{\log x}G_mA(1;\theta(x)).$$ The coefficient in this formula was defined by the finite arithmetic expressions <a href="#eq:AH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:AH">[eq:AH]</a>; the count $V$ has been used to establish their convergence and bounds, not to define them.

Corollary <a href="#cor:fixed-scaling" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:fixed-scaling">18</a> supplies the remaining assertion of the theorem for every $c>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:companion"></a>
### Least preimages

We now prove Theorem <a href="#thm:companion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:companion">2</a>. The prime that dominates a basic representation also determines the interval in which its least preimage lies. Counting that prime first gives the weight $f_k$. The resulting approximation is additive; a separate argument will show when its coefficient is bounded away from zero.

Throughout this section, $k\geq1$ is a fixed integer. The mass $M_f(x;H)$ is defined in <a href="#eq:mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass">[eq:mass]</a>; its summands correspond to distinct data $(d,p_1,\ldots,p_R)$, regardless of how many basic witnesses yield the same data. Write $$f_k(r)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&1\leq r\leq k,\\
  1-k/r,&k<r<k+1,\\
  1/r,&r\geq k+1.
 \end{cases}$$ Thus $0\leq f_k\leq1/(k+1)$, and $f_k(r)>0$ exactly when $r>k$. Equivalently, $f_k(r)$ is the length of $$\{t\in[0,1]:k<rt\leq k+1\}.$$ It is therefore the permitted fraction of the normalized value interval when the least-preimage ratio is fixed at $r$.

<a id="pm-h-45"></a>
#### Counting the interval for the largest prime

<a id="stmt-lem-weighted-endpoint"></a>
<div id="lem:weighted-endpoint" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:weighted-mass"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *For each sufficiently large fixed $H$, as $x$ tends to infinity, $$\label{eq:weighted-mass}
 \left|N_k(x)-\frac{x}{\log x}M_{f_k}(x;H)\right|
 \leq\bigl(\epsilon_{H,k}+o_{x\to\infty;H,k}(1)\bigr)
       \frac{xG_m}{\log x},$$ where $\epsilon_{H,k}\geq0$ tends to zero as $H\to\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-weighted-endpoint"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-weighted-endpoint</summary>

*Proof.* Proposition <a href="#prop:unique-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:unique-prefix">16</a> allows us, with an error of $(\epsilon_H+o_{x\to\infty;H}(1))xG_m/\log x$, to count tuples $(p_0,\ldots,p_R,d)$ satisfying $$(p_0-1)\cdots(p_R-1)d\leq x,
 \qquad
 kx<p_0\cdots p_R\ell(d)\leq(k+1)x.$$ Both the exceptional values and the tuples above them are controlled there. This use of the least-preimage identity requires that every preimage have the same prefix. The identity would not follow from the existence of a single basic witness.

<a id="eq:largest-prime-interval"></a>
Fix the remaining data $(d,p_1,\ldots,p_R)$, and put $$D=d\prod_{i=1}^R(p_i-1),\qquad
 A=\ell(d)\prod_{i=1}^Rp_i,\qquad
 r=\frac{\ell(d)}d,\qquad
 q=\prod_{i=1}^R\left(1-\frac1{p_i}\right).$$ Here $r\geq1$. Set $T=x/D$ and $U=x/A=Tq/r$. The admissible largest primes satisfy exactly $$\label{eq:largest-prime-interval}
 p_0\geq x^{0.9},\qquad
 kU<p_0\leq\min\{T+1,(k+1)U\}.$$ Once the lower bound for $p_0$ holds, the basic-witness restrictions on the remaining data do not change with $p_0$; recall that their zeroth simplex coordinate is $B$.

<a id="eq:weight-perturbation"></a>
Ignoring the lower cutoff and replacing $T+1$ by $T$, the interval has length $$\min\{T,(k+1)U\}-\min\{T,kU\}=T f_k(r/q).$$ The replacement changes its length by at most one. Moreover, $z\mapsto f_k(1/z)$ is $(k+1)$-Lipschitz on $[0,1]$, where its value at zero is zero. Consequently $$\label{eq:weight-perturbation}
 |f_k(r/q)-f_k(r)|
 \leq\frac{(k+1)(1-q)}r
 \leq(k+1)\sum_{i=1}^R\frac1{p_i}.$$

For fixed $H$, the deterministic size bounds in <a href="#eq:cofactor-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cofactor-size">[eq:cofactor-size]</a> give $T=x^{1-o(1)}$ uniformly in the remaining data. The tail has a witness $w$ bounded in terms of $H$ only, so $\ell(d)\leq w$ bounds $r$ in terms of $H$. The prime bands bound $q$ away from zero. Hence $U=x^{1-o(1)}$ uniformly as well, and $kU>x^{0.9}$ for all sufficiently large $x$. The lower cutoff in <a href="#eq:largest-prime-interval" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:largest-prime-interval">[eq:largest-prime-interval]</a> is then inactive.

Apply the prime number theorem at its two endpoints, taking the positive part of the difference if the interval is empty. Each nonzero endpoint is $x^{1-o(1)}$, and each is $O_k(T)$. Thus this gives, uniformly in the remaining data, $$\#\{p_0\text{ satisfying \eqref{eq:largest-prime-interval}}\}
 =\frac{T}{\log x}
   \left(f_k(r)+O_k\left(\sum_{i=1}^R\frac1{p_i}\right)
                +o_{x\to\infty;H,k}(1)\right).$$ The shift by one is included in the last error. This is an additive estimate relative to $T/\log x$, obtained from the ordinary prime number theorem; no relative estimate on a short prime interval is required.

The geometric separation of the bands implies $$\sup\sum_{i=1}^R\frac1{p_i}
       \ll \exp(-cH\rho^{-H}).$$ Indeed $b_{i-1}/b_i\geq\rho^{-1}$, and $p_i\geq\exp\exp(c b_i)$; the resulting reciprocal series is bounded by a constant times its smallest-scale bound. In particular, this estimate is uniform in the growing number $R$ of primes. Since $M_1\ll K_HG_m$ and $K_H=H^{o(1)}$, summing the displayed prime count gives an error bounded by $$\left(O_k\bigl(K_H\exp(-cH\rho^{-H})\bigr)
           +o_{x\to\infty;H,k}(1)\right)\frac{xG_m}{\log x}.$$ Together with the tuple-to-value error this proves <a href="#eq:weighted-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-mass">[eq:weighted-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

The additive form is necessary at this stage. For example, when $r=k$ and $q<1$, one has $f_k(r)=0$ but $f_k(r/q)>0$. Inequality <a href="#eq:weight-perturbation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weight-perturbation">[eq:weight-perturbation]</a> controls this boundary without asserting a relative approximation to a zero term.

<a id="eq:companion-additive"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:coefficient-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coefficient-convergence">22</a> with the function $$C_{f_k}(x)=\frac{N_k(x)\log x}{xG_m},$$ which is independent of $H$. Lemma <a href="#lem:weighted-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:weighted-endpoint">23</a> provides its required comparison with $M_{f_k}/G_m$. We obtain the uniform convergence of $A_H(f_k;s)$ on $0\leq s<1$ and $$\label{eq:companion-additive}
 N_k(x)=\frac{xG_m}{\log x}
          \bigl(A(f_k;\theta)+o(1)\bigr).$$ It remains to distinguish a coefficient bounded away from zero from an identically zero count.

<a id="pm-h-46"></a>
#### Seed propagation and bounded ratios

The positivity argument needs two distinct facts. A seed with $\ell(d)/d>k$ must produce a positive proportion of values whose ratios stay away from $k$. These ratios must also remain bounded on a positive proportion of values, since $f_k(r)\to0$ as $r\to\infty$. The next two lemmas supply these facts.

Preservation of a complete inverse fiber under multiplication by a prime already appears in Erdős’s proof of Theorem 4 (Erdős 1958) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Erdos1958](#ref-Erdos1958)]. Ford’s construction gives such preservation on a positive proportion of the distinct-totient scale. A later simultaneous two-seed form appears in Pollack, Pomerance and Treviño (Pollack, Pomerance, and Treviño 2013, Lemma 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[PPT2013](#ref-PPT2013)], again using Ford’s construction. We use the following single-seed consequence of Ford’s proof.

<a id="stmt-lem-fiber-propagation"></a>
<div id="lem:fiber-propagation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Ford’s inverse-fiber propagation). *Fix a totient $d$, and write $\varphi^{-1}(d)=\{d_1,\ldots,d_\kappa\}$. There are constants $\eta_d>0$ and $x_d$ such that, for every $x\geq x_d$, at least $\eta_dV(x)$ distinct totients $v\leq x$ have the form $$v=d\varphi(b),\qquad
 \varphi^{-1}(v)=\{bd_1,\ldots,bd_\kappa\}$$ for some positive integer $b$. In particular, these values satisfy $\ell(v)/v\geq\ell(d)/d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-fiber-propagation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-fiber-propagation</summary>

*Proof.* Use the construction in the proof of Theorem 2 of (Ford 1998, sec. 5, pp. 24–26, Equations (5.10)–(5.19)) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[FordTotients2013](#ref-FordTotients2013)]. For the fixed seed $d$, Ford constructs a set $\mathcal B$ and excludes at most half its members. For each remaining $b$, every preimage of $d\varphi(b)$ is one of the products $bd_i$. The paragraph following Equation (5.18) also proves that these members give distinct totients. The concluding bound on p. 26 is $\lvert\mathcal B\rvert/2\gg_\varepsilon d^{-1-\varepsilon}V(x)$ for sufficiently large $x$. This full-fiber conclusion is recorded explicitly in (Ford 1998, sec. 7.3, p. 39) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[FordTotients2013](#ref-FordTotients2013)]. Finally, $$\frac{\ell(v)}v
    =\frac{b}{\varphi(b)}\frac{\ell(d)}d
    \geq\frac{\ell(d)}d.$$ ◻

</details>

We also need to keep these ratios in a bounded interval. This follows from the existence of a bounded arithmetic tail and does not require uniqueness of the prefix.

<a id="stmt-lem-ratio-tightness"></a>
<div id="lem:ratio-tightness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 25**. *For every $\varepsilon>0$, there are $C<\infty$ and $x_0$ such that $$\#\{v\in\mathcal V:v\leq x,\ \ell(v)/v>C\}
     \leq\varepsilon V(x)\qquad(x\geq x_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-ratio-tightness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-ratio-tightness</summary>

*Proof.* Choose one sufficiently large fixed cut $H_0$ in Proposition <a href="#prop:basic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:basic">5</a>, so that its exceptional set has at most $\varepsilon V(x)$ members for all sufficiently large $x$. Every remaining value has a basic witness $n=p_0\cdots p_{R_0}w$, where $R_0=m-H_0$, $w<p_{R_0}$, and $w$ is bounded in terms of $H_0$ only. The prefix primes are distinct, and their bands give a uniform bound for $\prod_{i=0}^{R_0}p_i/(p_i-1)$. Therefore $$\frac{\ell(v)}v\leq\frac{n}{\varphi(n)}
   =\frac{w}{\varphi(w)}
      \prod_{i=0}^{R_0}\frac{p_i}{p_i-1}\leq C(H_0).$$ The last bound is independent of $x$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### The positive and zero alternatives

Suppose first that there is a totient $d_*$ with $\ell(d_*)>kd_*$. Choose $\delta>0$ such that $\ell(d_*)/d_*>k+2\delta$. Lemma <a href="#lem:fiber-propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:fiber-propagation">24</a> gives a fixed $\eta>0$ such that at least $\eta V(x)$ values satisfy $\ell(v)/v>k+2\delta$ for every sufficiently large $x$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:ratio-tightness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:ratio-tightness">25</a> with $\varepsilon=\eta/4$. It supplies a fixed $C$ for which at least $3\eta V(x)/4$ values satisfy <a id="eq:bounded-positive-ratios"></a>$$\label{eq:bounded-positive-ratios}
 k+2\delta<\frac{\ell(v)}v\leq C.$$ The numbers $\eta,\delta,C$ are fixed before the cut $H$ is allowed to grow.

For every sufficiently large $H$, and then sufficiently large $x$, Proposition <a href="#prop:unique-prefix" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:unique-prefix">16</a> removes at most $\eta V(x)/4$ of these values. For each remaining value its unique tuple satisfies $$\frac{\ell(v)}v=\frac{\ell(d)}d
                 \prod_{i=0}^R\frac{p_i}{p_i-1}.$$ The product is $1+O(\epsilon_H)+o_{x\to\infty;H}(1)$, uniformly in the tuple. Increasing $H$ and then $x$, we may bound it by $(k+2\delta)/(k+\delta)$. Thus at least $\eta V(x)/2$ distinct tuples have their tail ratio in the fixed interval $$I=[k+\delta,C].$$

<a id="eq:weighted-mass-positive"></a>
Let $M_{\mathbf 1_I}$ denote the mass <a href="#eq:mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass">[eq:mass]</a> with weight $\mathbf 1_I(r)$. The ordinary prime number theorem, counting all admissible largest primes for data whose tail ratio lies in $I$, bounds the number of such tuples above by $$\bigl(1+o_{x\to\infty;H}(1)\bigr)
       \frac{x}{\log x}M_{\mathbf 1_I}(x;H).$$ Comparison with $\eta V(x)/2$, followed by <a href="#eq:ford-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-scale">[eq:ford-scale]</a>, gives $$M_{\mathbf 1_I}(x;H)\geq c_1G_m$$ for all sufficiently large $H$ and then sufficiently large $x$, where $c_1>0$ is independent of $H$. Since $$\inf_{r\in I}f_k(r)
      \geq\min\left\{\frac{\delta}{k+\delta},\frac1C\right\}>0,$$ we conclude that $$\label{eq:weighted-mass-positive}
 M_{f_k}(x;H)\geq c_2G_m$$ with $c_2>0$ independent of sufficiently large $H$.

We transfer this lower bound first to the normalized count $C_{f_k}(x)=N_k(x)\log x/(xG_m)$, which does not depend on $H$. Fix a sufficiently large $H$ such that the error $\epsilon_{H,k}$ in <a href="#eq:weighted-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-mass">[eq:weighted-mass]</a> is less than $c_2/4$, and then take $x$ large enough that its remaining error is less than $c_2/4$. Equations <a href="#eq:weighted-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-mass">[eq:weighted-mass]</a> and <a href="#eq:weighted-mass-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-mass-positive">[eq:weighted-mass-positive]</a> give $C_{f_k}(x)\geq c_2/2$ for every sufficiently large $x$. This count is also bounded above, since $N_k(x)\leq V(x)$ and <a href="#eq:ford-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-scale">[eq:ford-scale]</a> holds. The bounds clause of Lemma <a href="#lem:coefficient-convergence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coefficient-convergence">22</a> therefore gives $$\inf_{0\leq s<1}A(f_k;s)\geq c_2/2>0.$$ Therefore the additive formula <a href="#eq:companion-additive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:companion-additive">[eq:companion-additive]</a> is multiplicative in this case. Its positive lower bound, the inequality $N_k(x)\leq V(x)$, and <a href="#eq:ford-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ford-scale">[eq:ford-scale]</a> give $N_k(x)\asymp_k V(x)$.

If instead $\ell(d)\leq kd$ for every totient $d$, then $\ell(v)\leq kv\leq kx$ for every $v\leq x$. Hence $N_k(x)=0$ for every $x$. Every weight $f_k(\ell(d)/d)$ in the finite arithmetic formula is also zero, so $A_H(f_k;s)=A(f_k;s)=0$. This proves the dichotomy in Theorem <a href="#thm:companion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:companion">2</a>. Its final assertion is established next.

<a id="stmt-rem-least-preimage-scope"></a>
<div id="rem:least-preimage-scope" class="remark">

**Remark 26**. The positive alternative occurs for $k=1$ and $k=2$. Ford gives $d=2^{18}\cdot257$ as a totient all of whose preimages are divisible by $8$ (Ford 1998, sec. 7.3, p. 39) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[FordTotients2013](#ref-FordTotients2013)]. Each such preimage $n$ is even and has an odd prime factor: a power of $2$ would have a power of $2$ as its totient. Thus $n/\varphi(n)=\prod_{p\mid n}p/(p-1)>2$, and $\ell(d)>2d$.

We do not determine all integers $k$ for which the positive alternative occurs. Its occurrence for every integer $k\geq1$ is equivalent to the unboundedness of $\ell(d)/d$ over totients $d$. Neither that unboundedness nor the complete classification of $k$ is established here.

</div>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The two cuts in a basic witness. We count $p_{0}$ by the prime number theorem and approximate the $p_{1}, …, p_{R}$ coordinates by volume. The later primes and the smooth integer $a$ remain discrete.",
      "figure_id": "fig:two-cuts",
      "pdf_page": 9,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:two-cuts",
          "exact_quote": "The two cuts in a basic witness. We count $p_0$ by the prime\nnumber theorem and approximate the $p_1,\\ldots,p_R$ coordinates by volume.\nThe later primes and the smooth integer $a$ remain discrete.",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small]\n \\node at (0.4,0.45) {$p_0$};\n \\node at (3.2,0.45) {$p_1\\ \\cdots\\ p_R$};\n \\node at (8,0.45) {$p_{R+1}\\ \\cdots\\ p_L$};\n \\node at (11.5,0.45) {$a$};\n \\draw[dashed] (5.55,-0.1)--(5.55,1.05);\n \\draw[dashed] (10.45,-0.1)--(10.45,1.05);\n \\node[above] at (5.55,1.05) {$R=m-H$};\n \\node[above] at (10.45,1.05) {$L=m-P$};\n \\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,mirror,amplitude=4pt}]\n       (0,-0.1)--(5.2,-0.1);\n \\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,mirror,amplitude=4pt}]\n       (5.9,-0.1)--(11.9,-0.1);\n \\node[below] at (2.6,-0.35) {prefix};\n \\node[below] at (8.9,-0.35) {finite arithmetic tail};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Horizontal schematic diagram illustrating the partitioning of the components of a basic witness: $p_0 \\quad p_1 \\cdots p_R \\quad p_{R+1} \\cdots p_L \\quad a$. Two vertical dashed cut lines extend downward from labels above: the first cut is labeled \"$R = m - H$\" between $p_R$ and $p_{R+1}$; the second cut is labeled \"$L = m - P$\" between $p_L$ and $a$. Below the sequence, horizontal underbraces designate groupings: an underbrace under $p_1 \\cdots p_R$ is labeled \"prefix\", and an underbrace spanning under $p_{R+1} \\cdots p_L \\quad a$ is labeled \"finite arithmetic tail\". The prime $p_0$ sits at the far left before the prefix."
    }
  ],
